Boundary behavior of functions in the Schur-Agler class of the polydisc Thanks: Partially supported by National Science Foundation Grants DMS 1361720 and 1665260, Heilbronn Institute for Mathematical Research (Focused Research Grant) and the Engineering and Physical Sciences Research Council grants EP/N03242X/1 and EP/T517914/1.
Jim Agler, Connor Evans, Zinaida A. Lykova, N. J. Young

Abstract
. We describe a generalization of the notion of a Hilbert space model of a function φ \varphi in the Schur-Agler class of the polydisc. This generalization is well adapted to the investigation of boundary behavior of φ \varphi at a mild singularity τ \tau on the d d -torus. We prove the existence of a generalized model with an enhanced continuity property at such a singularity τ \tau . We use this result to prove the directional differentiability of a function φ \varphi in the Schur-Agler class at a singular point on the d d -torus for which the Carathéodory condition holds and to calculate the corresponding directional derivative. The results of this paper extend to the polydisc 𝔻 d \mathbb{D}^{d} results of Agler, McCarthy, Tully-Doyle and Young which generalized to the bidisc the classical Julia-Wolff-Carathéodory theorem about analytic self-maps of 𝔻 \mathbb{D} .



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
32A30, 32S05, 47A56, 47A57

1.
Introduction

In this paper we prove the existence of directional derivatives of certain analytic functions
\varphi
on the polydisc
\mathbb{D}^{d}
, where
d
is a positive integer, at points of the boundary of
\mathbb{D}^{d}
where a weak regularity property is satisfied. To do this we use a generalization of the notion of a Hilbert-space model of
\varphi
.
Our results can be considered as higher-dimensional analogs of the Julia-Carathéodory
1
1

1

In the Julia-Wolff-Carathéodory theorem, the contribution of Wolff related to attractive fixed points of analytic self-maps of the disc. In this paper we are not concerned with self-maps of a domain nor with fixed points, so we shall refer to the classical result which we are generalizing as the “Julia-Carathéodory theorem”.
theorem about analytic self-maps of
\mathbb{D}
. First we generalize theorems of Agler, McCarthy and Young
[
3
]
on Carathéodory theorems for the bidisc via Hilbert space methods, and secondly we extend theorems proved by Agler, Tully-Doyle and Young in
[
5
]
on the boundary behavior of analytic functions on the bidisc.

Definition 1.1
.

[
2
]

Let
\varphi:\mathbb{D}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
be analytic. A model of
\varphi
on
\mathbb{D}^{d}
is a pair
(\mathcal{L},v)
, where
\mathcal{L}
is a separable complex Hilbert space with an orthogonal decomposition
\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{L}_{1}\oplus\ldots\oplus\mathcal{L}_{d}
and
v:\mathbb{D}^{d}\rightarrow\mathcal{L}
is an analytic map such that, for all
\lambda,\mu\in\mathbb{D}^{d}
,

1-\overline{\varphi(\mu)}\varphi(\lambda)=\Big\langle{\big(1_{\mathcal{L}}-\mu_{P}^{*}\lambda_{P}\big)v({\lambda}),v({\mu})}\Big\rangle_{\mathcal{L}},

(1.1)

where
P
is the
d
-tuple
P=(P_{1},\ldots,P_{d})
on
\mathcal{L}
, for
j=1,\ldots,d
,
P_{j}:\mathcal{L}\rightarrow\mathcal{L}
is the orthogonal projection onto
\mathcal{L}_{j}
, and
\lambda_{P}
denotes the operator
\lambda_{1}P_{1}+\ldots+\lambda_{d}P_{d}
.

The
Schur class

\mathcal{S}(U)
of a domain
U
in
\mathbb{C}^{d}
is defined to be the set of analytic functions
\varphi
on
U
such that
|\varphi(z)|\leq 1
for all
z\in U
. The following result from
[
2
]
shows that models have been an effective tool for the Schur class functions on the bidisc.

Theorem 1.2
.

Let
\varphi:\mathbb{D}^{2}\to\mathbb{C}
be analytic. The following conditions are equivalent.

(1)

\varphi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{D}^{2})
;

(2)

\varphi
has a model;

(3)

\varphi
satisfies Ando’s inequality, that is,
for all commuting pairs
(T_{1},T_{2})
of contractions on a complex Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
,

\sup_{r<1}\|\varphi(rT_{1},rT_{2})\|_{\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})}\leq\sup_{\lambda\in\mathbb{D}^{2}}|\varphi(\lambda)|.

In this paper, for any complex Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
, we denote by
\mathcal{B(\mathcal{H})}
the Banach algebra of bounded linear operators
T:\mathcal{H}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}
, with composition as multiplication and the operator norm.

Agler also proved in
[
2
]
that, for any positive integer
d
and for any analytic function
\varphi
on
\mathbb{D}^{d}
,
\varphi
has a model if and only if
\varphi
satisfies the
d
-dimensional analog of von Neumann’s inequality, that is, conditions (2) and (3) of Theorem
1.2
(with the obvious modifications for functions of
d
variables) are equivalent for all
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{D}^{d})
when
d\geq 2
. It is well known that the
d
-dimensional analog of von Neumann’s inequality fails for some functions from
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{D}^{d})
, when
d\geq 3
, see
[
22
,
11
]
.
That is, conditions (1) and (3) of Theorem
1.2
are not equivalent for all
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{D}^{d})
when
d>2
.
Therefore, not all functions
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{D}^{d})
have models when
d\geq 3
.
It is natural to consider the class of functions for which the analog of von Neumann’s inequality does hold.

Definition 1.3
.

[
2
]

The Schur-Agler class, denoted by
\mathcal{SA}_{d}
, is the set of analytic functions
\varphi:\mathbb{D}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
such that, for all commuting
d
-tuples
(T_{1},\ldots,T_{d})
of contractions acting on any complex Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
,

\|\varphi\|_{(d,\infty)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}\sup_{r<1}\|\varphi(rT_{1},\ldots,rT_{d})\|_{\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})}\leq 1.

It was proved in
[
2
]
that the analytic functions on
\mathbb{D}^{d}
that have models are precisely the functions in

\mathcal{SA}_{d}
.

Since functions in the Schur-Agler class of the polydisc have models,
it is natural to use models to elucidate the function-theoretic properties of such functions and, in particular, to study the boundary behavior of such functions near a mild singularity. By a
singularity
of a function
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{D}^{d})
we mean a point
\tau
in the topological boundary
\partial\mathbb{D}^{d}
of the polydisc such that
\varphi
does not extend analytically to any neighborhood of
\tau
. We say that a function
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{D}^{d})

satisfies the Carathéodory condition at a point
\tau
in
\partial\mathbb{D}^{d}

if

\liminf_{\lambda\to\tau,\lambda\in\mathbb{D}^{d}}\frac{1-|\varphi(\lambda)|}{1-\|\lambda\|_{\infty}}<\infty.

(1.2)

When this relation holds we also say that
\tau
is a carapoint for

\varphi
.

Definition 1.4
.

(Non-tangential approach on
\mathbb{D}^{d}
)

Let
S\subseteq\mathbb{D}^{d}
and
\tau\in\partial\mathbb{D}^{d}
be such that
\tau\in\overline{S}
. If there exists a constant
c>0
such that, for all
\lambda\in S
,

\|\lambda-\tau\|_{\infty}\leq c(1-\|\lambda\|_{\infty}),

then we say
S
approaches
\tau
non-tangentially, denoted by
S{\overset{nt}{\rightarrow}}\tau
.

The classical Julia-Carathéodory Theorem
[
9
]
, which Carathéodory proved in 1929, states:

Theorem 1.5
.

Let
\tau\in\mathbb{T}
and let
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{D})
. The following conditions are equivalent.

(1)

\tau
is a carapoint for
\varphi
;

(2)

the quantity

\sup_{\lambda\in S}\dfrac{1-\lvert\varphi(\lambda)\rvert}{1-\lvert\lambda\rvert}<\infty

whenever
S\subseteq\mathbb{D}
and
S{\overset{nt}{\rightarrow}}\tau
;

(3)

\varphi
is directionally differentiable at
\tau
, that is,
there exists a complex number
\omega
such that,
whenever
\delta\in\mathbb{C}
and

\tau+t\delta\in\mathbb{D}^{d}
for sufficiently small positive
t
,

\lim_{t\rightarrow 0^{+}}\dfrac{\varphi(\tau+t\delta)-\omega}{t}~~{\text{exists}}.

Moreover,
|\omega|=1
;

(4)

\varphi
is non-tangentially differentiable at
\tau
, that is, there exist
\omega\in\mathbb{C}
and
\eta\in\mathbb{C}
such that

\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\rightarrow\tau\\
\lambda\in S\end{subarray}}\dfrac{\varphi(\lambda)-\omega-\eta(\lambda-\tau)}{\lvert\lambda-\tau\rvert}=0

whenever
S\subset\mathbb{D}
and
S{\overset{nt}{\rightarrow}}\tau
. Moreover,
|\omega|=1
;

(5)

\varphi
is non-tangentially continuously differentiable at
\tau
, that is, condition

(4)
holds and, in addition,

\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\rightarrow\tau\\
\lambda\in S\end{subarray}}\varphi^{\prime}(\lambda)=\eta

whenever
S\subseteq\mathbb{D}
and
S{\overset{nt}{\rightarrow}}\tau
.

The gist of Theorem
1.5
is
that if a function satisfies the Carathéodory condition at a point
\tau\in\mathbb{T}
then it satisfies some apparently stronger regularity conditions there, in particular, it has an “angular derivative” at
\tau
(meaning that condition (4) holds).

Carathéodory’s result has been extended to functions of several variables by numerous authors, notably by K. Włodarczyk
[
23
]
, W. Rudin
[
20
]
, F. Jafari
[
14
]
, M. Abate
[
1
]
and by Agler, McCarthy and Young
[
3
]
. A version of the Julia-Carathéodory theorem for functions in the Schur-Agler class of the polydisc was proved by J.A. Ball and V. Bolotnikov in
[
7
, Lemma 2.1]
.

In particular, in
[
3
]
Theorem
1.5
was extended to analytic functions on the bidisc
\mathbb{D}^{2}
. For
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{D}^{2})
and for

\tau\in\partial\mathbb{D}^{2}
, the authors proved that if
\varphi
satisfies the Carathéodory condition at
\tau
then the directional derivatives
D_{\delta}\varphi(\tau)
exist for all appropriate directions
\delta\in\mathbb{C}^{2}
.
They also give an example of a rational inner function
\psi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{D}^{2})
,

\psi:\mathbb{D}^{2}\to\mathbb{D}
, given by the formula

\psi(\lambda)=\frac{2\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}}{2-\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}},

which satisfies Carathéodory’s condition at the point
\chi:=(1,1)
but
is not non-tangentially differentiable at
\chi
. In
[
5
]
Agler, Tully-Doyle and Young introduced the notion of a “generalized model”, which enabled them to present an alternative, more algebraic, proof of these results.

In this paper we analyse the boundary behavior of functions in the Schur-Agler class
\mathcal{SA}_{d}
of the polydisc
\mathbb{D}^{d}
of any dimension
d\geq 2
. We exploit Hilbert space methods which were introduced in
[
5
]
for analytic functions on the bidisc
\mathbb{D}^{2}
.
We shall show below that, for a function
\varphi\in\mathcal{SA}_{d}
, the Carathéodory condition at a point
\tau\in\mathbb{T}^{d}
implies that
\varphi
has a non-tangential limit at
\tau
, and that
\varphi
is directionally differentiable at
\tau
.

An essential component of our approach will be the notion of a generalized model, which we now define.

Definition 1.6
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a Hilbert space and let

I:\mathbb{D}^{d}\mapsto\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})

be an operator-valued analytic function. We say that
I
is inner if, for almost all
\lambda\in\mathbb{T}^{d}
with respect to Lebesgue measure, the radial limit

\lim_{r\rightarrow 1^{-}}I(r\lambda)

exists with respect to the strong operator topology and is an isometric operator.

The following definition, an extension of Definition
1.1
in which
\lambda_{P}
is replaced by a general analytic operator-valued function
I
, will be central to our efforts.

Definition 1.7
.

Let
\varphi:\mathbb{D}^{d}\to\mathbb{C}
be analytic. The triple
(\mathcal{M},u,I)
is a generalized model of
\varphi
if

(1)

\mathcal{M}
is a separable Hilbert space,

(2)

u:\mathbb{D}^{d}\to\mathcal{M}
is analytic on
\mathbb{D}^{d}
,

(3)

I:\mathbb{D}^{d}\rightarrow\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{M})
is analytic on
\mathbb{D}^{d}
and
\|I(\lambda)\|_{\mathcal{B(M)}}<1
for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{D}^{d}
, and

(4)

the equation

1-\overline{\varphi(\mu)}\varphi(\lambda)=\Big\langle{\big(1_{\mathcal{M}}-I(\mu)^{*}I(\lambda)\big)u(\lambda),u(\mu)}\Big\rangle_{\mathcal{M}}

(1.3)

holds for all
\lambda,\mu\in\mathbb{D}^{d}
.

A generalized model
(\mathcal{M},u,I)
is inner if
I(\cdot)
is an inner
\mathcal{B(\mathcal{M})}
-valued function on

\mathbb{D}^{d}
.

This simple relaxation of the definition of model enables us to concentrate information about singular behavior of
\varphi
in the inner function
I(\cdot)
rather than the model function
u
. This fact proved efficacious in
[
5
]
for the description of directional derivatives at a carapoint
\tau
for
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{D}^{2})
since one obtains a generalized model in which the function
u:\mathbb{D}^{d}\to\mathcal{M}
extends continuously to the singular point
\tau
.
True, the cost is an inner operator-valued function
I(\cdot)
which is, in principle, more complicated than the function
\lambda_{P}
. However, it transpires that one can construct a generalized model in which the inner operator-valued function
I(\cdot)
is given by an explicit (and fairly simple) formula.
In this paper we carry out a similar programme to that of
[
5
]
in dimensions
d\geq 2
.
We formalise the appropriate continuity property of
u
in the following definition.

Definition 1.8
.

[
5
, Definition 3.4]

Let
(\mathcal{M},u,I)
be a generalized model of a function
\varphi\in\mathcal{SA}_{d}
. A point
\tau\in\partial\mathbb{D}^{d}
is a
C
-point
of the model
(\mathcal{M},u,I)
if, for every subset
S
of
\mathbb{D}^{d}
that approaches
\tau
nontangentially,
u
extends continuously to
S\cup\{\tau\}
, with respect to the norm topology on
\mathcal{M}
.

We shall interpret
\mathbb{C}^{d}
as a Banach algebra with co-ordinate-wise operations and identity
\mathds{1}=(1,1,\ldots,1)
so that, for example, if
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{d})
, then

\bigg(\dfrac{\mathds{1}}{\mathds{1}-\lambda}\bigg)=\bigg(\dfrac{1}{1-\lambda_{1}},\ldots,\dfrac{1}{1-\lambda_{d}}\bigg).

Following Definition
1.2
, we define carapoints of
operator-valued
functions on
\mathbb{D}^{d}
.

Definition 1.9
.

Let
I
be a contractive operator-valued analytic function on
\mathbb{D}^{d}
. We say that
\tau\in\mathbb{T}^{d}
is a carapoint for
I
if

\liminf_{\lambda\to\tau}\frac{1-\|I(\lambda)\|}{1-\|\lambda\|_{\infty}}<\infty.

Definition 1.10
.

Corresponding to a function
\varphi\in\mathcal{SA}_{d}
and a carapoint
\tau\in\mathbb{T}^{d}
of
\varphi
, we define a desingularized model of
\varphi
relative to
\tau
to be a generalized model
(\mathcal{M},u,I)
of
\varphi
such that the following conditions hold:

(1)

\tau
is a
C
-point for
(\mathcal{M},u,I)
;

(2)

\tau
is a carapoint for
I
and
\lim_{\lambda{\overset{nt}{\rightarrow}}\tau}I(\lambda)=1_{\mathcal{M}}
;

(3)

I:\mathbb{D}^{d}\rightarrow\mathcal{B(M)}
is an inner
\mathcal{B(M)}
-valued function.

The main tool we shall use in this paper is the following theorem (see Theorem
8.2
).

Theorem 1.11
.

Let
\tau\in\mathbb{T}^{d}
be a carapoint for
\varphi\in\mathcal{SA}_{d}
. There exists a
desingularized model
(\mathcal{M},u,I)
of
\varphi
relative to
\tau
.
Furthermore, we can express
I
in the form
2
2

2

Here the notation
\left(\frac{\mathds{1}}{\mathds{1}-\bar{\tau}\lambda}\right)_{Y}^{-1}
denotes
\left(\frac{1}{1-\bar{\tau}_{1}\lambda_{1}}Y_{1}+\dots+\frac{1}{1-\bar{\tau}_{d}\lambda_{d}}Y_{d}\right)^{-1}
.

I(\lambda)=1_{\mathcal{M}}-\bigg(\dfrac{\mathds{1}}{\mathds{1}-\overline{\tau}\lambda}\bigg)_{Y}^{-1},\ \text{for}\ \lambda\in\mathbb{D}^{d},

(1.4)

for some
d
-tuple of positive contractions
(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{d})
on
\mathcal{M}
such that
\sum_{j=1}^{d}Y_{j}=1_{\mathcal{M}}
. At every point
\lambda\in\mathbb{T}^{d}
such that
\lambda_{j}\neq\tau_{j}
for
j=1,\ldots,d
,
I(\cdot)
extends analytically to
\lambda
and
\|I(\lambda)\|_{\mathcal{B(M)}}=1
.

We now address directional derivatives at
\varphi\in\mathcal{SA}_{d}
at a carapoint
\tau\in\mathbb{T}^{d}
. The fact that
\tau
is a carapoint for
\varphi\in\mathcal{SA}_{d}
implies that a non-tangential limit at
\tau
,

\lim_{\lambda{\overset{nt}{\rightarrow}}\tau}\varphi(\lambda)=\omega,

exists, and its value
\omega
satisfies
|\omega|=1
(see Corollary
8.3
).
For
\tau\in\mathbb{T}^{d}
, one can see that
\delta
from
\mathbb{C}^{d}
points into
\mathbb{D}^{d}
at
\tau
if and only if
-\delta\in\mathbb{C}^{d}_{+}(\tau)
, where

\mathbb{C}_{+}^{d}(\tau)=\big\{(z_{1},\ldots,z_{d})\in\mathbb{C}^{d}:\mathrm{Re}(z_{j}\overline{\tau}_{j})>0~~\text{for}~~j=1,\ldots,d\big\}.

Definition 1.12
.

Let
\varphi\in\mathcal{SA}_{d}
and let
\tau\in\mathbb{T}^{d}
be a carapoint for
\varphi
. We say that

\varphi
is directionally differentiable at
\tau
if
there exists a complex number

\omega
such that, whenever
-\delta\in\mathbb{C}^{d}_{+}(\tau)
,

\lim_{t\rightarrow 0^{+}}\dfrac{\varphi(\tau+t\delta)-\omega}{t}~~{\text{exists}}.

In Section
9
the following results are proved by the use of desingularized models of
\varphi\in\mathcal{SA}_{d}
(see Theorem
9.1
).

Theorem 1.13
.

If
\tau\in\mathbb{T}^{d}
is a carapoint for
\varphi\in\mathcal{SA}_{d}
, then
\varphi
is directionally differentiable at
\tau
.

Definition 1.14
.

Let
\varphi\in\mathcal{SA}_{d}
and let
\tau\in\mathbb{T}^{d}
be a carapoint for
\varphi
. Let
\delta\in\mathbb{C}^{d}
point into
\mathbb{D}^{d}
at
\tau
. We say that
\varphi
is differentiable in the direction
\delta
at the point
\tau
if there exists a complex number
\omega
(necessarily
|\omega|=1
) such that

\lim_{t\rightarrow 0^{+}}\dfrac{\varphi(\tau+t\delta)-\omega}{t}~~{\text{exists}}.

When the limit exists we denote it by

D_{\delta}\varphi(\tau).

Theorem
9.1
in the paper gives also a formula for the directional derivative
D_{\delta}\varphi(\tau)
of
\varphi
at
\tau
.

Theorem 1.15
.

Let
\tau\in\mathbb{T}^{d}
be a carapoint for
\varphi\in\mathcal{SA}_{d}
.
There exists an analytic function
h:\mathbb{C}_{+}^{d}(\tau)\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
such that

(1)

\mathrm{Re}(-h(z))>0
for all
z\in\mathbb{C}_{+}^{d}(\tau)
;

(2)

h(\tau)=-\liminf_{\lambda{\rightarrow}\tau}\dfrac{1-\lvert\varphi(\lambda)\rvert}{1-\|\lambda\|_{\infty}};

(1.5)

(3)

for all
\delta\in\mathbb{C}_{+}^{d}(\tau)
,

D_{-\delta}\varphi(\tau)=\varphi(\tau)h(\delta),

(1.6)

where
\varphi(\tau)=\lim_{\lambda{\overset{nt}{\rightarrow}}\tau}\varphi(\lambda)
and
|\varphi(\tau)|=1
.

Moreover, we can express the function
h:\mathbb{C}_{+}^{d}(\tau)\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
by

h(z)=-\bigg\langle\bigg(\dfrac{\mathds{1}}{\overline{\tau}z}\bigg)_{Y}^{-1}u(\tau),u(\tau)\bigg\rangle_{\mathcal{M}}\ ~~\text{for all}~z\in\mathbb{C}_{+}^{d}(\tau),

for some desingularized model
(\mathcal{M},u,I)
of
\varphi
with
I
of the form

I(\lambda)=1_{\mathcal{M}}-\bigg(\dfrac{\mathds{1}}{\mathds{1}-\overline{\tau}\lambda}\bigg)_{Y}^{-1}\ \text{for}\ \lambda\in\mathbb{D}^{d},

(1.7)

for some
d
-tuple of positive contractions
(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{d})
on
\mathcal{M}
such that
\sum_{j=1}^{d}Y_{j}=1_{\mathcal{M}}
.

We formalise the appropriate boundedness property of
u
in the following definition.

Definition 1.16
.

[
5
, Definition 3.4]
(
B
-point of the model
)
Let
(\mathcal{L},u)
be a model of a function
\varphi\in\mathcal{SA}_{d}
. A point
\tau\in\mathbb{T}^{d}
is a
B
-point of the model if
u
is bounded on every subset of
\mathbb{D}^{d}
that approaches
\tau
nontangentially.

To illustrate the results of Theorem
1.15
, let us consider the function

\varphi_{3}:\mathbb{D}^{3}\to\mathbb{D}
given by the formula

\varphi_{3}(\lambda)=\frac{3\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}\lambda_{3}-\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{2}\lambda_{3}-\lambda_{3}\lambda_{1}}{3-\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{3}}.

This function is a rational inner function on the tridisc
\mathbb{D}^{3}
. G. Knese showed by an explicit construction
[
17
, Theorem 2.11]
that
\varphi_{3}
belongs to the Schur-Agler class of
\mathbb{D}^{3}
. It is easy to see that
\varphi_{3}
has the radial limit
-1
at the point
\tau\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}(1,1,1)
in
\mathbb{T}^{3}
, and we shall show in the Section
10
that
\tau
is a singularity of
\varphi_{3}
, in the sense that
\varphi_{3}
does not extend analytically, or even continuously, to
\mathbb{D}^{3}\cup\{\tau\}
. Despite this singularity at
\tau
,
\varphi_{3}
does satisfy the Carathéodory condition at
\tau
. In Section
10
we present an explicit
9
-dimensional model
(\mathcal{L},v)
of
\varphi_{3}
. For this model
(\mathcal{L},v)
,
\tau
is a
B
-point, but not a
C
-point. The proof of Theorem
1.11
shows how then to compute a desingularized model
(\mathcal{M},u,I)
for
\varphi_{3}
for which
\tau
is a
C
-point, so that
u(\tau)
is a well-defined vector
\alpha\in\mathcal{M}
, say. We prove that, for all directions

-\delta
that point into
\mathbb{D}^{3}
at
\tau
,
\varphi_{3}
has a directional derivative

D_{-\delta}\varphi_{3}(\tau)=\varphi_{3}(\tau)h(\delta),\ \text{where}\ \ h(\delta)=-\bigg\langle\bigg(\dfrac{\mathds{1}}{\overline{\tau}\delta}\bigg)_{Y}^{-1}\alpha,\alpha\bigg\rangle_{\mathcal{M}},

for some
3
-tuple of positive contractions
Y=(Y_{1},Y_{2},Y_{3})
on
\mathcal{M}
for which
Y_{1}+Y_{2}+Y_{3}=1_{\mathcal{M}}
.

A well-known lurking isometry argument proceeds from a model
(\mathcal{M},u)
of a function
\varphi\in\mathcal{SA}_{d}
to a realization of
\varphi

[
2
]
. The identical argument applied to a generalized model
(\mathcal{M},u,I)
produces a generalized notion of realization.

Theorem 1.17
.

If
(\mathcal{L},u,I)
is a generalized model of
\varphi\in\mathcal{SA}_{d}
then there exist a Hilbert space
\mathcal{M}
containing
\mathcal{L}
, a scalar
a\in\mathbb{C}
, vectors
\beta,\gamma\in\mathcal{M}
and a linear operator
D:\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{M}
such that the operator

L=\begin{bmatrix}a&1\otimes\beta\\
\gamma\otimes 1&D\end{bmatrix}

(1.8)

is unitary on
\mathbb{C}\oplus\mathcal{M}
and, for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{D}^{d}
,

L\begin{pmatrix}1\\
I(\lambda)u(\lambda)\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\varphi(\lambda)\\
u(\lambda)\end{pmatrix},

(1.9)

and consequently, for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{D}^{d}
,

\varphi(\lambda)=a+\left\langle I(\lambda)(1_{\mathcal{M}}-DI(\lambda))^{-1}\gamma,\beta\right\rangle_{\mathcal{M}}.

(1.10)

Proof.

By equation (
1.3
), for all
\lambda,\mu\in\mathbb{D}^{d}
,

1+\left\langle I(\lambda)u(\lambda),I(\mu)u(\mu)\right\rangle_{\mathcal{L}}=\overline{\varphi(\mu)}\varphi(\lambda)+\left\langle u(\lambda),u(\mu)\right\rangle_{\mathcal{L}}.

We may interpret this equation as an equality between the gramians of two families of vectors in
\mathbb{C}\oplus\mathcal{L}
.
Accordingly we may define an isometric operator

L_{0}:\spa\left\{\begin{pmatrix}1\\
I(\lambda)u(\lambda)\end{pmatrix}:\lambda\in\mathbb{D}^{d}\right\}\to\spa\left\{\begin{pmatrix}\varphi(\lambda)\\
u(\lambda)\end{pmatrix}:\lambda\in\mathbb{D}^{d}\right\}

by the equation

L_{0}\begin{pmatrix}1\\
I(\lambda)u(\lambda)\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\varphi(\lambda)\\
u(\lambda)\end{pmatrix}.

(1.11)

If necessary we may enlarge
\mathbb{C}\oplus\mathcal{L}
to a space
\mathbb{C}\oplus\mathcal{M}
in which the domain and range of
L_{0}
have equal codimension, and then we may extend
L_{0}
to a unitary operator
L
on
\mathbb{C}\oplus\mathcal{M}
.
Let us represent
L
as a 2 x 2 block matrix,

L=\begin{bmatrix}a&1\otimes\beta\\
\gamma\otimes 1&D\end{bmatrix}:\mathbb{C}\oplus\mathcal{M}\to\mathbb{C}\oplus\mathcal{M},

then

\begin{pmatrix}\varphi(\lambda)\\
u(\lambda)\end{pmatrix}=L\begin{pmatrix}1\\
I(\lambda)u(\lambda)\end{pmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}a&1\otimes\beta\\
\gamma\otimes 1&D\end{bmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1\\
I(\lambda)u(\lambda)\end{pmatrix}\ \text{for all}\ \lambda\in\mathbb{D}^{d}.

(1.12)

For each
\lambda\in\mathbb{D}^{d}
, we define
I(\lambda)
to be equal to
0
on
\mathcal{M}\ominus\mathcal{L}
, so that
I(\lambda)
becomes an element of
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{M})
.
In particular, by the equality (
1.12
), for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{D}^{d}
,

\displaystyle\varphi(\lambda)

\displaystyle=a+\langle I(\lambda)u(\lambda),\beta\rangle_{\mathcal{M}},

(1.13)

\displaystyle\gamma

\displaystyle=(1_{\mathcal{M}}-DI(\lambda))u(\lambda).

(1.14)

As
L
is unitary,
D
is a contraction and, by assumption (3) of Definition
1.7
,
\|I(\lambda)\|_{\mathcal{B(\mathcal{M})}}<1
for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{D}^{d}
, and so
(1_{\mathcal{M}}-DI(\lambda))
is invertible for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{D}^{d}
.
Consequently, we may solve the last equation for
u(\lambda)
to obtain the equation

u(\lambda)=(1_{\mathcal{M}}-DI(\lambda))^{-1}\gamma\ \text{for all}\ \lambda\in\mathbb{D}^{d}.

Therefore, for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{D}^{d}
,

\varphi(\lambda)=a+\left\langle I(\lambda)(1_{\mathcal{M}}-DI(\lambda))^{-1}\gamma,\beta\right\rangle_{\mathcal{M}}.

(1.15)

∎

The ordered 4-tuple
(a,\beta,\gamma,D)
, as in equation (
1.8
), will be called a
realization
of the (generalized) model
(\mathcal{L},u,I)
of
\varphi
if
L
is a contraction and equation (
1.9
) holds. It will be called a
unitary realization
if in addition
L
is unitary on
\mathbb{C}\oplus\mathcal{L}
.

The paper is organized as follows. In Section
2
we discuss the Carathéodory condition and study properties of functions
\varphi\in\mathcal{SA}_{d}
at
B
-points and at
C
-points. We prove numerous results concerning the boundary behavior of a function
\varphi\in\mathcal{SA}_{d}
. These results allow us to move seamlessly between results formulated in terms of functions, to their equivalents restated in terms of models. These re-statements make such functions more amenable to analysis.
In Section
5
we prove some technical results about operator-valued analytic functions of a particular form on
\mathbb{D}^{d}
which will be used in our proof the main theorem (Theorem
1.11
).
In Section
6
we investigate conditions on invertibility and analyticity of the operator-valued functions on
\mathbb{D}^{d}
given by

(\mathds{1}-\lambda)_{T}~\text{and}~\bigg(\dfrac{\mathds{1}}{\mathds{1}-\lambda}\bigg)_{T}

for a
d
-tuple of operators
T=(T_{1},\ldots,T_{d})
on
\mathcal{M}
.
In Section
8
we prove the main result of our paper Theorem
1.11
. Starting with a model
(\mathcal{L},v)
of
\varphi\in\mathcal{SA}_{d}
with a
B
-point
\tau
, we arrive at a model
(\mathcal{M},u,I)
of a more general type. It is called the desingularization of the model
(\mathcal{L},v)
at
\tau
, see Definition
1.10
, and satisfies additional conditions, in particular,
\tau
is a
C
-point for
(\mathcal{M},u,I)
and
\lim_{\lambda{\overset{nt}{\rightarrow}}\tau}I(\lambda)=1_{\mathcal{M}}
.
In Section
9
the model
(\mathcal{M},u,I)
is utilised to prove the directional differentiability of a function
\varphi\in\mathcal{SA}_{d}
at a carapoint
\tau
for suitable directions into the polydisc, see Theorem
1.15
.

2.
Special types of singularities for Schur-Agler class functions

In this section we follow the paper “A Carathéodory Theorem for the bidisc via Hilbert space methods” by Agler, McCarthy and Young,
[
3
]
. The authors of
[
3
]
proved a Julia-Carathéodory Theorem for the Schur class functions on
\mathbb{D}^{2}
. To make this paper self-contained, in this section, we extend their results to the class
\mathcal{SA}_{d}
. We must first make suitable definitions of carapoints,
B
-points,
C
-points and angular gradients which permit the formulation of analogues of the main results of
[
3
]
and allow the proofs to remain valid, with minimal changes. To investigate the boundary behaviour of a function
\varphi\in\mathcal{SA}_{d}
by means of models, we require the definitions of carapoints,
B
-points and
C
-points of a model for the given function
\varphi
.

We use the notation
\|\cdot\|_{\infty}
to denote the
l^{\infty}
norm on
\mathbb{C}^{d}
; that is, for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}^{d}
,

\|\lambda\|_{\infty}=\max\{\lvert\lambda_{1}\rvert,\ldots,\lvert\lambda_{d}\rvert\}.

Although we mostly concern ourselves with points
\tau\in\mathbb{T}^{d}
, we include for completeness their companion results for

\partial\mathbb{D}^{d}:=\big\{z\in\mathbb{C}^{d}:\lvert z_{j}\rvert\leq 1~\text{for}~j=1,\ldots,d~\text{and}~\lvert z_{j}\rvert=1~\text{for at least one}~j=1,\ldots,d\big\}.

Definition 2.1
.

(
Julia quotient for
\mathbb{D}^{d}
)

Let
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{D}^{d})
. The Julia quotient of
\varphi
, denoted
J_{\varphi}
, is the function on
\mathbb{D}^{d}
given by

J_{\varphi}({\lambda})=\dfrac{1-\lvert{\varphi(\lambda)}\rvert}{1-\|{\lambda}\|_{\infty}}.

Note here and throughout, we use the superscript notation
\lambda^{(n)}
to denote an element of a sequence
(\lambda^{(n)})_{n=1}^{\infty}
. If
(\lambda^{(n)})_{n=1}^{\infty}
is a sequence in
\mathbb{D}^{d}
, then we use the subscript notation
\lambda^{(n)}_{j}
to denote the
j
th
component of the point
\lambda^{(n)}
.

Definition 2.2
.

(
Non-tangential approach on
\mathbb{D}^{d}
)

Let
S\subseteq\mathbb{D}^{d}
and
\tau\in\partial\mathbb{D}^{d}
be such that
\tau\in\overline{S}
. If there exists a constant
c>0
such that, for all
\lambda\in S
,

\|\lambda-\tau\|_{\infty}\leq c(1-\|\lambda\|_{\infty}),

then we say
S
approaches
\tau
non-tangentially, denoted
S{\overset{nt}{\rightarrow}}\tau
. Similarly, if
(\lambda^{(n)})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is a sequence in
\mathbb{D}^{d}
satisfying
\lambda^{(n)}\rightarrow\tau
and, for some
c>0
,

\|\lambda^{(n)}-\tau\|_{\infty}\leq c(1-\|\lambda^{(n)}\|_{\infty}),

(2.1)

for all
n\geq 1
, then we say that
\lambda^{(n)}
has non-tangential limit
\tau
. We denote this relation by
\lambda^{(n)}{\overset{nt}{\rightarrow}}\tau
.

The notion of carapoints essentially remains the same, but with
\lambda\in\mathbb{D}^{d}
instead of
\lambda\in\mathbb{D}
and now the Julia quotient of Definition
2.1
.

Definition 2.3
.

(
Carapoints for
\varphi
on
\mathbb{D}^{d}
)

Let
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{D}^{d})
and let
\tau\in\partial\mathbb{D}^{d}
. We say
\tau
is a carapoint for
\varphi
if

\liminf_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\rightarrow\tau\\
\lambda\in\mathbb{D}^{d}\end{subarray}}J_{\varphi}(\lambda)<\infty,

or equivalently, there exists a sequence
\{\lambda^{(n)}\}
in
\mathbb{D}^{d}
such that
\lambda^{(n)}\rightarrow\tau
and

\sup_{n}J_{\varphi}(\lambda^{(n)})<\infty.

The following definition is the natural analogue of a non-tangentially differentiable function on the unit disc for a function on the polydisc.

Definition 2.4
.

(
Angular gradient
)

Let
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{D}^{d})
and let
\tau\in\mathbb{T}^{d}
be a carapoint of
\varphi
. We say that
\varphi
has an angular gradient at
\tau\in\mathbb{C}^{d}
if there exist
\omega\in\mathbb{C}
of unit modulus and
\eta\in\mathbb{C}^{d}
such that

\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\rightarrow\tau\\
\lambda\in S\end{subarray}}\dfrac{\varphi(\lambda)-\omega-\eta\cdot(\lambda-\tau)}{\|\lambda-\tau\|_{\infty}}=0

whenever
S\subset\mathbb{D}^{d}
and
S{\overset{nt}{\rightarrow}}\tau
. Here

\eta\cdot(\lambda-\tau)=\eta_{1}(\lambda_{1}-\tau_{1})+\ldots+\eta_{d}(\lambda_{d}-\tau_{d})

and the angular gradient of
\varphi
at
\tau
is the vector
\begin{bmatrix}\eta_{1}\\
\vdots\\
\eta_{d}\end{bmatrix}.

In Proposition
4.1
, we will establish the connection between a “
B
-point of
\varphi
” and a “
B
-point of a model of
\varphi
”.

Definition 2.5
.

(
B
-point of
\varphi
)

Let
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{D}^{d})
and let
\tau\in\partial\mathbb{D}^{d}
. We say that
\tau
is a
B
-point for
\varphi
if there exists a sequence
\{\lambda^{(n)}\}
in
\mathbb{D}^{d}
converging to
\tau
such that

\sup_{n}\dfrac{1-\lvert{\varphi(\lambda^{(n)})}\rvert}{1-\|\lambda^{(n)}\|_{\infty}}<\infty.

Example 2.6
.

[
3
, Page 602]
(
B
-point for a function
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{D}^{2})
)

Consider the rational inner function

\varphi(\lambda)=\dfrac{\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{1}+\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}}{1-\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{1}-\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{2}},~\lambda\in\mathbb{D}^{2}.

It was shown in
[
3
, Section 6]
that
(1,1)
is a
B
-point for

\varphi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{D}^{2})
,
\varphi
has non-tangential limit
1
at
(1,1)
and does not extend continuously to
\mathbb{D}^{2}\cup\{(1,1)\}
. Despite these facts, the directional derivative
D_{\delta}\varphi((1,1))
exists for every direction
\delta

pointing into the bidisc at
(1,1)
.

Definition 2.7
.

[
5
, Definition 3.4]
(
B
-point of the model
)
Let
(\mathcal{M},u)
be a model of a function
\varphi\in\mathcal{SA}_{d}
. A point
\tau\in\mathbb{T}^{d}
is a
B
-point of the model if
u
is bounded on every subset of
\mathbb{D}^{d}
that approaches
\tau
nontangentially.

Definition 2.8
.

(
C
-point of
\varphi
)

Let
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{D}^{d})
, let
\tau\in\mathbb{T}^{d}
be a carapoint of
\varphi
and suppose
\varphi
has a nontangential limit at
\tau
. When
\varphi
has an angular gradient at
\tau
, we say that
\tau
is a
C
-point of
\varphi
.

Example 2.9
.

[
3
, Page 588]
(
C
-point for a function
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{D}^{2})
)
Consider the rational function

\varphi(\lambda)=\dfrac{-4\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}^{2}+\lambda_{2}^{2}+3\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}}{\lambda_{2}^{2}-\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{1}-3\lambda_{2}+4},~\lambda\in\mathbb{D}^{2}.

It was shown in
[
3
]
that
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{D}^{2})
and
\varphi
has a
C
-point at
(1,1)\in\mathbb{T}^{2}
.

Definition 2.10
.

[
5
, Definition 3.4]
(
C
-point of the model
)

Let
(\mathcal{M},u)
be a model of a function
\varphi\in\mathcal{SA}_{d}
.
A point
\tau
is a
C
-point of the model if, for every subset
S
of
\mathbb{D}^{d}
that approaches
\tau
nontangentially,
u
extends continuously to
S\cup\{\tau\}
, with respect to the norm topology on
\mathcal{M}
.

For models on the disc, if
\tau\in\mathbb{T}
is a
B
-point for
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{D})
, then for all
\lambda^{(n)}{\overset{nt}{\rightarrow}}\tau
,
u(\lambda^{(n)})
tends weakly to the unique limit in our model space,
[
4
, Proposition 5.30]
. For the bidisc and upwards, this is no longer the case, as the set of limits of sequences
u(\lambda^{(n)})
as
\lambda^{(n)}{\overset{nt}{\rightarrow}}\tau
is potentially larger than a single vector,
[
4
, Page 147]
. Because of this technicality, we shall require the following notion, similar to that found in
[
3
, Section 4]
.

Definition 2.11
.

(
Cluster Set
)

We define the cluster set of a model
(\mathcal{M},u)
for a function
\varphi\in\mathcal{SA}_{d}
at a
B
-point
\tau
to be the set of weak limits in
\mathcal{M}
of the weakly convergent sequences
\{u(\lambda^{(n)})\}
as
\{\lambda^{(n)}\}
ranges over all sequences in
\mathbb{D}^{d}
that tend to
\tau
and satisfy

\sup_{n}J_{\varphi}(\lambda^{(n)})<\infty.

(2.2)

We denote the cluster set at
\tau
by
Y_{\tau}
.

Theorem 2.12
.

[
4
, Theorem 9.103]
(
Realization formula for the Schur-Agler class
)

A function
\varphi
belongs to
\mathcal{SA}_{d}
if and only if there exists a decomposed Hilbert space
\mathcal{M}
, a scalar
a\in\mathbb{C}
, vectors
\beta,\gamma\in\mathcal{M}
and a contraction
D
on
\mathcal{M}
such that

V=\begin{bmatrix}a&1\otimes\beta\\
\gamma\otimes 1&D\end{bmatrix}:\mathbb{C}\oplus\mathcal{M}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}\oplus\mathcal{M}

is a unitary operator and

\varphi(\lambda)=a+\langle\lambda_{P}(1_{\mathcal{M}}-D\lambda_{P})^{-1}\gamma,\beta\rangle_{\mathcal{M}}

(2.3)

for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{D}^{d}
, where
P
is the
d
-tuple
P=(P_{1},\ldots,P_{d})
on
\mathcal{M}
,
P_{j}:\mathcal{M}\rightarrow\mathcal{M}
is the orthogonal projection onto
\mathcal{M}_{j}
for
j=1,\ldots,d
and
\lambda_{P}
denotes the operator
\lambda_{1}P_{1}+\ldots+\lambda_{d}P_{d}
on
\mathcal{M}
. Moreover, for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{D}^{d}
,

\displaystyle\gamma

\displaystyle=(1_{\mathcal{M}}-D\lambda_{P})u(\lambda)

(2.4)

\displaystyle\varphi(\lambda)

\displaystyle=a+\langle\lambda_{P}u(\lambda),\beta\rangle_{\mathcal{M}}.

(2.5)

Remark 2.13
.

Let
(\mathcal{M},u)
be a model of
\varphi\in\mathcal{SA}_{d}
, see Definition
1.1
.
By equation (
1.1
), for
\mu=\lambda\in\mathbb{D}^{d}
,

\displaystyle 1-\lvert\varphi(\lambda)\rvert^{2}

\displaystyle=\Big\langle{\big(1_{\mathcal{M}}-\lambda_{P}^{*}\lambda_{P}\big)u({\lambda}),u({\lambda})}\Big\rangle_{\mathcal{M}}

\displaystyle=\Big\langle{\big(1_{\mathcal{M}}-\sum_{i=1}^{d}\overline{\lambda_{i}}P_{i}\cdot\sum_{j=1}^{d}\lambda_{j}P_{j}\big)u({\lambda}),u({\lambda})}\Big\rangle_{\mathcal{M}}.

(2.6)

Recall that, by Definition
1.1
, each
P_{j}:\mathcal{M}\rightarrow\mathcal{M}
is the orthogonal projection onto
\mathcal{M}_{j}
for
j=1,\ldots,d
. We have
P_{i}^{*}=P_{i}
and
P_{i}P_{j}=0
for every
i\neq j
and
P_{j}^{2}=P_{j}
for all
i,j=1,\ldots,d
. We shall denote
P_{j}u(\lambda)
by
u_{j}(\lambda)
for all
j=1,\ldots,d
and
\lambda\in\mathbb{D}^{d}
. Hence

\sum_{i=1}^{d}\overline{\lambda_{i}}P^{*}_{i}\cdot\sum_{j=1}^{d}\lambda_{j}P_{j}=\sum_{j=1}^{d}\lvert\lambda_{j}\rvert^{2}P_{j},

and thus, for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{D}^{d}
,

\displaystyle 1-\lvert\varphi(\lambda)\rvert^{2}

\displaystyle=\Big\langle{\big(1_{\mathcal{M}}-\sum_{j=1}^{d}\lvert\lambda_{j}\rvert^{2}P_{j}\big)u({\lambda}),u({\lambda})}\Big\rangle_{\mathcal{M}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{d}\Big\langle(1-\lvert\lambda_{j}\rvert^{2})P_{j}u_{j}({\lambda}),u_{j}({\lambda})\Big\rangle_{\mathcal{M}_{j}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{d}(1-\lvert\lambda_{j}\rvert^{2})\|u_{j}(\lambda)\|^{2}_{\mathcal{M}_{j}}.

(2.7)

We shall use equation (
2.7
) extensively throughout this paper.

3.
Boundary results for Schur-Agler functions

Proposition 3.1
.

Let
\tau\in\partial\mathbb{D}^{d}
be a
B
-point for
\varphi\in\mathcal{SA}_{d}
, let
(\mathcal{M},u)
be a model of
\varphi
and let
Y_{\tau}
be the cluster set of the model

(\mathcal{M},u)
at
\tau
.

(1)

If
x\in Y_{\tau}
and
\lvert\tau_{j}\rvert<1
for any
j=1,\ldots,d
, then
x_{j}=0
.

(2)

There exists
\omega\in\mathbb{T}
such that, for all
x\in Y_{\tau}
and
\lambda\in\mathbb{D}^{d}
,

1-\overline{\omega}\varphi(\lambda)=\sum_{\lvert\tau_{j}\rvert=1}\Big(1-\overline{\tau}_{j}\lambda_{j}\Big)\langle u_{j}(\lambda),x_{j}\rangle_{\mathcal{M}_{j}}.

(3.1)

(3)

If

\liminf_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda{\overset{nt}{\rightarrow}}\tau\\
\lambda\in\mathbb{D}^{d}\end{subarray}}\dfrac{1-\lvert\varphi(\lambda)\rvert}{1-\|\lambda\|_{\infty}}=\alpha,

then there exists an
x\in Y_{\tau}
such that
\|x\|^{2}_{\mathcal{M}}\leq\alpha
.

Proof.

(2) Consider
x\in Y_{\tau}
. Pick a sequence
\{\lambda^{(n)}\}
in
\mathbb{D}^{d}
converging to
\tau
such that
u(\lambda^{(n)})\rightarrow x
weakly and relation (
2.2
) holds. Passing to a subsequence if necessary, we can guarantee that
\varphi(\lambda^{(n)})\rightarrow\omega
for some
\omega\in\overline{\mathbb{D}}
. Since
x\in Y_{\tau}
, the relation (
2.2
) holds, and we have
\lvert\omega\rvert=1
. For the model
(\mathcal{M},u)
of
\varphi
, let
\mu=\lambda^{(n)}
as
n\rightarrow\infty
in equation (
1.1
) to obtain

1-\overline{\omega}\varphi(\lambda)=\sum_{j=1}^{d}(1-\overline{\tau}_{j}\lambda_{j})\langle u_{j}(\lambda),x_{j}\rangle_{\mathcal{M}_{j}}.

(3.2)

If
\tau\notin\mathbb{T}^{d}
, say
\lvert\tau_{j}\rvert<1
for some
j=1,\ldots,d
, then on setting
\lambda=\lambda^{(n)}
in equation (
3.2
) and letting
n\rightarrow\infty
we find that

1-\lvert\omega\rvert^{2}=0=\sum_{j=1}^{d}(1-\lvert\tau_{j}\rvert^{2})\|x_{j}\|^{2}_{\mathcal{M}_{j}}.

Therefore
x_{j}=0
whenever
\tau_{j}\in\mathbb{D}
. This proves statement (1) and equation (
3.1
) holds for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{D}^{d}
. Suppose also that
y\in Y_{\tau}
is such that
u(\mu_{n})\rightarrow y
where
\mu_{n}\rightarrow\tau
and
\varphi(\mu_{n})\rightarrow\zeta
. Put
\lambda=\mu_{n}
in equation (
3.1
) and let
n\rightarrow\infty
to obtain
1-\overline{\omega}\zeta=0
, that is,
\omega=\zeta
. Thus
\omega
is uniquely determined and is independent of the choice of
x\in Y_{\tau}
, and so (2) holds.

(3) Pick a sequence
\{\lambda^{(n)}\}
in
\mathbb{D}^{d}
converging to
\tau
such that

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\dfrac{1-\lvert\varphi(\lambda^{(n)})\rvert}{1-\|\lambda^{(n)}\|_{\infty}}=\alpha.

(3.3)

Since
\tau
is a
B
-point of
(\mathcal{M},u)
,
\alpha<\infty
. Passing to a subsequence if necessary, we can suppose that
\varphi(\lambda^{(n)})
converges to a point
\omega\in\overline{\mathbb{D}}
. Equation (
3.3
) implies that
\omega\in\mathbb{T}
. Since
1-\lvert\lambda^{(n)}_{j}\rvert^{2}\geq 1-\|\lambda^{(n)}\|^{2}_{\infty}
, for all
j=1,2,\dots
,

\displaystyle\dfrac{1-\lvert\varphi(\lambda^{(n)})\rvert^{2}}{1-\|\lambda^{(n)}\|_{\infty}^{2}}

\displaystyle=\dfrac{1}{1-\|\lambda^{(n)}\|_{\infty}^{2}}\Big\langle(1_{\mathcal{M}}-\lambda_{P}^{*}\lambda_{P})u(\lambda^{(n)}),u(\lambda^{(n)})\Big\rangle_{\mathcal{M}}

\displaystyle=\dfrac{1}{1-\|\lambda^{(n)}\|_{\infty}^{2}}\sum_{j=1}^{d}\big(1-\lvert\lambda^{(n)}_{j}\rvert^{2}\big)\|u_{j}(\lambda^{(n)})\|_{\mathcal{M}}^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{j=1}^{d}\|u_{j}(\lambda^{(n)})\|_{\mathcal{M}}^{2}=\|u(\lambda^{(n)})\|_{\mathcal{M}}^{2}.

Thus,
\|u(\lambda^{(n)})\|_{\mathcal{M}}
is bounded. By the compactness and metrizability of closed balls in
\mathcal{M}
in the weak topology, we can take a further subsequence to arrange that
u(\lambda^{(n)})
converges weakly to some
x\in\mathcal{M}
, where
\|x\|_{\mathcal{M}}^{2}\leq\alpha
. Clearly
x\in Y_{\tau}
and equation (
3.1
) holds.∎

The following theorem
will be essential to prove equivalence of various definitions of a
B
-ponit
\tau
for
\varphi\in\mathcal{SA}_{d}
.

Theorem 3.2
.

Let
\varphi\in\mathcal{SA}_{d}
,
\tau\in\partial\mathbb{D}^{d}
. If there is a sequence
\{\lambda^{(n)}\}
in
\mathbb{D}^{d}
converging to
\tau
such that

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\dfrac{1-\lvert\varphi(\lambda^{(n)})\rvert}{1-\|\lambda^{(n)}\|_{\infty}}=\alpha<\infty,

then there exists
\omega\in\mathbb{T}
such that, for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{D}^{d}
,

\dfrac{\lvert\varphi(\lambda)-\omega\rvert^{2}}{1-\lvert\varphi(\lambda)\rvert^{2}}\leq\alpha\max_{\lvert\tau_{j}\rvert=1}\dfrac{\lvert\lambda_{j}-\tau_{j}\rvert^{2}}{1-\lvert\lambda_{j}\rvert^{2}}.

(3.4)

Proof.

We will prove the inequality for
\tau\in\mathbb{T}^{d}
, as by the considerations of Proposition
3.1
, statement (1), the conclusion then follows for
\tau\in\partial\mathbb{D}^{d}
.

Pick a model
(\mathcal{M},u)
of
\varphi
. By Proposition
3.1
, there exist an
\omega\in\mathbb{T}
and an
x\in Y_{\tau}
such that

\|x\|^{2}_{\mathcal{M}}\leq\alpha,

(3.5)

and equation (
3.1
) holds for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{D}^{d}
. If
\alpha=0
then
x=0
and so, by equation (
3.1
),

1-\overline{\omega}\varphi(\lambda)=0\iff\varphi(\lambda)=\omega.

Therefore
\varphi(\lambda)
is the constant function
\omega
and so inequality (
3.4
) holds when
\alpha=0
.
Consider a fixed
\lambda\in\mathbb{D}^{d}
. From equation (
3.1
)
we have

\displaystyle\lvert\varphi(\lambda)-\omega\rvert^{2}

\displaystyle=\lvert 1-\overline{\omega}\varphi(\lambda)\rvert^{2}

\displaystyle=\Bigg\lvert\sum_{j=1}^{d}\big\langle(1-\overline{\tau}_{j}\lambda_{j})u_{j}(\lambda),x_{j}\big\rangle_{\mathcal{M}_{j}}\Bigg\rvert^{2}

\displaystyle=\Bigg\lvert\Bigg\langle\begin{bmatrix}(1-\overline{\tau}_{1}\lambda_{1})u_{1}(\lambda)\\
\vdots\\
(1-\overline{\tau}_{d}\lambda_{d})u_{d}(\lambda)\\
\end{bmatrix},\begin{bmatrix}x_{1}\\
\vdots\\
x_{d}\end{bmatrix}\Bigg\rangle_{\mathcal{M}}\Bigg\rvert^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\Bigg\|\begin{bmatrix}(1-\overline{\tau}_{1}\lambda_{1})u_{1}(\lambda)\\
\vdots\\
(1-\overline{\tau}_{d}\lambda_{d})u_{d}(\lambda)\\
\end{bmatrix}\Bigg\|^{2}_{\mathcal{M}}\Bigg\|\begin{bmatrix}x_{1}\\
\vdots\\
x_{d}\end{bmatrix}\Bigg\|^{2}_{\mathcal{M}}

\displaystyle=\bigg(\sum_{j=1}^{d}\|(1-\overline{\tau}_{j}\lambda_{j})u_{j}(\lambda)\|_{\mathcal{M}_{j}}^{2}\bigg)\bigg(\sum_{j=1}^{d}\|x_{j}\|_{\mathcal{M}_{j}}^{2}\bigg).

Thus

\displaystyle\lvert\varphi(\lambda)-\omega\rvert^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\|x\|_{\mathcal{M}}^{2}\sum_{j=1}^{d}\lvert 1-\overline{\tau}_{j}\lambda_{j}\rvert^{2}\|u_{j}(\lambda)\|_{\mathcal{M}_{j}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\|x\|_{\mathcal{M}}^{2}\sum_{j=1}^{d}\lvert\tau_{j}-\lambda_{j}\rvert^{2}\dfrac{1-\lvert\lambda_{j}\rvert^{2}}{1-\lvert\lambda_{j}\rvert^{2}}\|u_{j}(\lambda)\|_{\mathcal{M}_{j}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\|x\|_{\mathcal{M}}^{2}\bigg(\max_{j=1,\ldots,d}\dfrac{\lvert\tau_{j}-\lambda_{j}\rvert^{2}}{1-\lvert\lambda_{j}^{2}\rvert}\bigg)\sum_{j=1}^{d}\Big({1-\lvert\lambda_{j}\rvert^{2}}\Big)\|u_{j}(\lambda)\|_{\mathcal{M}_{j}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\|x\|_{\mathcal{M}}^{2}\bigg(\max_{j=1,\ldots,d}\dfrac{\lvert\tau_{j}-\lambda_{j}\rvert^{2}}{1-\lvert\lambda_{j}^{2}\rvert}\bigg)(1-\lvert\varphi(\lambda)\rvert^{2}),

where the last line in the above equation is derived from equation (
2.7
). Hence by dividing through by
1-\lvert\varphi(\lambda)\rvert^{2}
and using the inequality (
3.5
) in the last equation above, we prove inequality (
3.4
).∎

We will need the language of horocycles and horospheres to formulate the following two corollaries to Theorem
3.2
.

Definition 3.3
.

[
3
]

A horocycle in
\mathbb{D}
is a set of the form
E(\tau,R)
for some
\tau\in\mathbb{T}
and
R>0
, where

E(\tau,R)=\bigg\{\lambda\in\mathbb{D}:\dfrac{\lvert\lambda-\tau\rvert^{2}}{1-\lvert\lambda\rvert^{2}}<R\bigg\}.

For
\tau\in\mathbb{D}^{d}
and
R>0
, the d-dimensional horosphere is defined to be the set

E(\tau,R)=E(\tau_{1},R)\times\ldots\times E(\tau_{d},R).

Let
D(z,r)
denote the Euclidean disc in
\mathbb{C}
with centre
z
and radius
r>0
. Then

E(\tau,R)=D\bigg(\dfrac{\tau}{R+1},\dfrac{R}{R+1}\bigg).

(3.6)

Note that
E(\tau_{j},R)
is the circular disc internally tangent to
\mathbb{T}
at
\tau_{j}
having radius
R/(R+1)
,
[
3
, Section 4]
.

Corollary 3.4
.

Let
\varphi\in\mathcal{SA}_{d}
,
\tau\in\partial\mathbb{D}^{d}
. If there is a sequence
\{\lambda^{(n)}\}
in
\mathbb{D}^{d}
converging to
\tau
such that

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\dfrac{1-\lvert\varphi(\lambda^{(n)})\rvert}{1-\|\lambda^{(n)}\|_{\infty}}=\alpha<\infty,

then there exists
\omega\in\mathbb{T}
such that, for any
R>0
,

\varphi(E(\tau,R))\subset E(\omega,\alpha R).

Proof.

Let
\lambda\in E(\tau_{1},R)\times\ldots\times E(\tau_{d},R)
, that is,

\dfrac{\lvert\lambda_{j}-\tau_{j}\rvert^{2}}{1-\lvert\lambda_{j}\rvert^{2}}<R~~\text{for all}~j=1,\ldots,d.

By Theorem
3.2
,

\dfrac{\lvert\varphi(\lambda)-\omega\rvert^{2}}{1-\lvert\varphi(\lambda)\rvert^{2}}\leq\alpha R.

Therefore
\varphi(\lambda)\in E(\omega,\alpha R)
.∎

The following statement allows us to test whether
\tau
is a
B
-point using only the values of
\varphi
along the radius through
\tau
.

Corollary 3.5
.

Let
\varphi\in\mathcal{SA}_{d}
and
\tau\in\partial\mathbb{D}^{d}
. The following conditions are equivalent:

(1)

\tau
is a
B
-point for
\varphi
;

(2)

\dfrac{1-\lvert\varphi(\lambda)\rvert}{1-\|\lambda\|_{\infty}}
is bounded on the radius
\{r\tau:0<r<1\}
;

(3)

\dfrac{1-\lvert\varphi(\lambda)\rvert}{1-\|\lambda\|_{\infty}}
is bounded on every set in
\mathbb{D}^{d}
that approaches
\tau
non-tangentially.

Moreover, if
\tau
is a
B
-point for
\varphi
,

\lim_{r\rightarrow 1^{-}}\dfrac{1-\lvert\varphi(r\tau)\rvert}{1-r}=\liminf_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\rightarrow\tau\\
\lambda\in\mathbb{D}^{d}\end{subarray}}\dfrac{1-\lvert\varphi(\lambda)\rvert}{1-\|\lambda\|_{\infty}}.

(3.7)

Proof.

It is easily seen that (3)
\implies
(2)
\implies
(1).

(1)
\implies
(3). Suppose
\tau
is a
B
-point of
\varphi
and consider a set
S\subset\mathbb{D}^{d}
that approaches
\tau
in such a way that
\|\lambda-\tau\|_{\infty}\leq c(1-\|\lambda\|_{\infty})
, for some
c\geq 1
. Let

\alpha=\liminf_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\rightarrow\tau\\
\lambda\in\mathbb{D}^{d}\end{subarray}}\dfrac{1-\lvert\varphi(\lambda)\rvert}{1-\|\lambda\|_{\infty}},

then by the first assumption,
\alpha
is finite. By Corollary
3.4
, there exists an
\omega\in\mathbb{T}
such that, for all
R>0
,

\varphi(E(\tau,R))\subset E(\omega,\alpha R)=D\bigg(\dfrac{\omega}{\alpha R+1},\dfrac{\alpha R}{\alpha R+1}\bigg).

(3.8)

Pick any
\lambda\in S
and
\epsilon>0
and let

R_{1}=(1+\epsilon)\dfrac{\|\lambda-\tau\|_{\infty}^{2}}{1-\|\lambda\|_{\infty}^{2}}.

Then
\lambda\in E(\tau,R_{1})
and therefore

0<R_{1}\leq(1+\epsilon)\dfrac{c^{2}(1-\|\lambda\|_{\infty})^{2}}{1-\|\lambda\|_{\infty}^{2}}\leq(1+\epsilon)c^{2}(1-\|\lambda\|_{\infty}).

(3.9)

By the relation (
3.8
),

\displaystyle 1-\lvert\varphi(\lambda)\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\lvert\varphi(\lambda)-\omega\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\lvert\varphi(\lambda)-\dfrac{\omega}{\alpha R_{1}+1}\rvert+\lvert\dfrac{\omega}{\alpha R_{1}+1}-\omega\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\dfrac{2\alpha R_{1}}{\alpha R_{1}+1}.

By inequality (
3.9
),

1-\lvert\varphi(\lambda)\rvert\leq\dfrac{2\alpha R_{1}}{\alpha R_{1}+1}\leq 2\alpha R_{1}\leq 2(1+\epsilon)\alpha c^{2}(1-\|\lambda\|_{\infty}).

Since the inequality above holds for all
\epsilon>0
, by dividing through by
(1-\|\lambda\|_{\infty})
, we have

\dfrac{1-\lvert\varphi(\lambda)\rvert}{1-\|\lambda\|_{\infty}}\leq 2\alpha c^{2}.

Hence (1)
\implies
(3).

We now prove equation (
3.7
). By Theorem
3.2
, there exists a
\omega\in\mathbb{T}
such that the inequality (
3.4
) holds for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{D}^{d}
. In particular, choosing
\lambda=r\tau
, we have

\dfrac{\lvert\varphi(r\tau)-\omega\rvert^{2}}{1-\lvert\varphi(r\tau)\rvert^{2}}\leq\alpha\dfrac{1-r}{1+r},

for
0\leq r<1
. This means that
\varphi(r\tau)
is in the horocycle
E(\omega,R)
where
R=\alpha(1-r)/(1+r)
. Since
1-\lvert\varphi(r\tau)\rvert\leq\lvert\varphi(r\tau)-\omega\rvert\leq 2R/(R+1)
,

\dfrac{1-\lvert\varphi(r\tau)\rvert}{1-r}\leq\dfrac{\lvert\varphi(r\tau)-\omega\rvert}{1-r}\leq\dfrac{2\alpha}{1+\alpha+r(\alpha-1)}.

(3.10)

Let

f(r)=\dfrac{2\alpha}{1+\alpha+r(\alpha-1)}.

We claim that
f(r)
is bounded for
0\leq r<1
. Note that
(1+\alpha)+r(\alpha-1)=0
if and only if
r=(1+\alpha)/(\alpha-1)
. If
\alpha>1
, then

\dfrac{1+\alpha}{\alpha-1}=\dfrac{\alpha-1+2}{\alpha-1}=1+\dfrac{2}{\alpha-1}>1.

If
0\leq\alpha<1
, then
\alpha-1<0
and so

\dfrac{1+\alpha}{\alpha-1}<0.

Hence, for
0\leq r<1
,
f(r)
is bounded, and inequality (
3.10
) is properly defined, since the denominator is non-zero. Taking limits of the right hand side of inequality (
3.10
),

\lim_{r\rightarrow 1^{-}}\dfrac{2\alpha}{1+r+\alpha(1-r)}=\alpha.

Thus we have

\alpha=\liminf_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\rightarrow\tau\\
\lambda\in\mathbb{D}^{d}\end{subarray}}\dfrac{1-\lvert\varphi(\lambda)\rvert}{1-\|\lambda\|_{\infty}}\leq\liminf_{r\rightarrow 1^{-}}\dfrac{1-\lvert\varphi(r\tau)\rvert}{1-r}\leq\limsup_{r\rightarrow 1^{-}}\dfrac{1-\lvert\varphi(r\tau)\rvert}{1-r}\leq\alpha.

Hence we prove equation (
3.7
). ∎

For the purposes of this section, we require another notion of nontangential approach to prove Corollary
3.7
.

Definition 3.6
.

[
3
, Section 2]

If
S\subseteq\mathbb{D}^{d}
, we say that
S
approaches
\tau
plurinontangentially if
\tau\in\overline{S}
and there exists sets
S_{1},\ldots,S_{d}\subseteq\mathbb{D}
such that
S_{j}
approaches
\tau_{j}
nontangentially for all
j=1,\ldots,d
, and
S\subseteq S_{1}\times\ldots\times S_{d}
. We denote a plurinontangential approach by
\lambda{\overset{pnt}{\rightarrow}}\tau
.

The
horospheric topology
(see
[
3
, Page 595]
) on the closed polydisc is the topology for which a base consists of all open sets of
\mathbb{D}^{d}
together with all sets of the form
\{\tau\}\cup E(\tau,R)
for
\tau\in\partial\mathbb{D}^{d}
and
R>0
.

Corollary 3.7
.

If
\varphi\in\mathcal{SA}_{d}
and
\tau\in\partial\mathbb{D}^{d}
is a
B
-point for
\varphi
then there exists
\omega\in\mathbb{T}
such that
\varphi(\lambda)\rightarrow\omega
as
\lambda{\overset{pnt}{\rightarrow}}\tau
in the horospheric topology.

Proof.

Pick
\omega\in\mathbb{T}
as in Corollary
3.4
. We must show that for every
\epsilon>0
, there exists a horospheric neighbourhood
E(\tau,R)
of
\tau
such that,

\varphi(E(\tau,R))\subset D(\omega,\epsilon).

(3.11)

In view of the inclusion of Corollary
3.4
, choose
r>0
such that
E(\omega,r)\subset D(\omega,\epsilon)
and then take
R=r/\alpha
. Thus
\varphi(\lambda)\rightarrow\omega
as
\lambda\rightarrow\tau
with respect to the horospheric topology.
Consider a net
(\lambda^{(n)})
in
\mathbb{D}^{d}
that tends to
\tau
plurinontangentially so that, for all
j=1,\ldots,d
, there is a set
S_{j}
that approaches
\tau_{j}
nontangentially such that all
\lambda^{(n)}_{j}\in S_{j}
and
\lambda^{(n)}\rightarrow\tau
.
We want to show that
\varphi(\lambda^{(n)})\rightarrow\omega
. Let
\epsilon>0
and choose
R>0
such that the relation (
3.11
) holds. We claim that there exists a
\delta>0
such that, for
j=1,\ldots,d

D(\tau_{j},z)\cap S_{j}\subset E(\tau_{j},R).

If
\tau_{j}\in\mathbb{D}
, then this inclusion is satisfied, since
E(\tau_{j},R)=\mathbb{D}
. Otherwise, if
S_{1},\ldots,S_{d}
have a plurinontangential approach for some
c>1
, it suffices to take any
z
such that

\delta<\min\bigg\{c,\dfrac{2Rc}{c^{2}+R}\bigg\}.

Since
\lambda^{(n)}{\overset{pnt}{\rightarrow}}\tau
, for large enough
n
we have
\lambda^{(n)}_{j}\in D(\tau_{j},z)\cap S_{j}\subset E(\tau_{j},R)
. Hence
\lambda^{(n)}\in E(\tau,R)
and so
\varphi(\lambda^{(n)})\in D(\omega,\epsilon)
as required.∎

4.
Connection between
B
-points for
\varphi
and realizations

Proposition
4.1
will give us a connection between a
B
-point of a function
\varphi\in\mathcal{SA}_{d}
and a
B
-point of the model
(\mathcal{M},u)
for
\varphi
.

Proposition 4.1
.

Let
\varphi\in\mathcal{SA}_{d}
,
\tau\in\partial\mathbb{D}^{d}
and
\{\lambda^{(n)}\}
be a sequence in
\mathbb{D}^{d}
such that
\lambda^{(n)}{\overset{nt}{\rightarrow}}\tau
. The following statements are equivalent.

(1)

\sup_{n}\dfrac{1-\lvert\varphi(\lambda^{(n)})\rvert}{1-\|\lambda^{(n)}\|_{\infty}}<\infty;

(2)

there exists a model
(\mathcal{M},u)
of
\varphi
such that
(u(\lambda^{(n)}))_{n=1}^{\infty}
is bounded in
\mathcal{M}
;

(3)

for every model
(\mathcal{M},u)
of
\varphi
,
(u(\lambda^{(n)}))_{n=1}^{\infty}
is bounded in
\mathcal{M}
.

Proof.

Fix a
c
such that
\|\tau-\lambda^{(n)}\|_{\infty}\leq c(1-\|\lambda^{(n)}\|_{\infty})
for all
n
. Clearly (3)
\implies
(2).

Assume (1). Let

\sup_{n}\dfrac{1-\lvert\varphi(\lambda^{(n)})\rvert}{1-\|\lambda^{(n)}\|_{\infty}}=M<\infty.

If
(\mathcal{M},u)
is a model of
\varphi
and

\|\lambda^{(n)}\|_{\infty}=\max_{j=1,\ldots,d}\{\lvert\lambda_{j}^{(n)}\rvert\}
, then, for
\lambda^{(n)}\in\mathbb{D}^{d}
,

\lvert\lambda^{(n)}_{j}\rvert^{2}\leq\lvert\lambda^{(n)}_{j}\rvert\leq\|\lambda^{(n)}\|_{\infty}~~\text{and}~~1-\lvert\lambda^{(n)}_{j}\rvert^{2}\geq 1-\lvert\lambda^{(n)}_{j}\rvert\geq 1-\|\lambda^{(n)}\|_{\infty}.

Thus

\displaystyle(1-\|\lambda^{(n)}\|_{\infty})\|u(\lambda^{(n)})\|_{\mathcal{M}}^{2}

\displaystyle=(1-\|\lambda^{(n)}\|_{\infty})\sum_{j=1}^{d}\|u_{j}(\lambda^{(n)})\|_{\mathcal{M}_{j}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=1}^{d}(1-\lvert\lambda^{(n)}_{j}\rvert^{2})\|u_{j}(\lambda^{(n)})\|_{\mathcal{M}_{j}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\bigg\langle(1_{\mathcal{M}}-(\lambda^{(n)})_{P}^{*}(\lambda^{(n)})_{P}u(\lambda^{(n)}),u(\lambda^{(n)})\bigg\rangle_{\mathcal{M}}.

By equation (
2.6
) and equation (
2.7
),

(1-\|\lambda^{(n)}\|_{\infty})\|u(\lambda^{(n)})\|_{\mathcal{M}}^{2}=1-\lvert\varphi(\lambda^{(n)})\rvert^{2}\leq 2M(1-\|\lambda^{(n)}\|_{\infty}).

Hence, if we divide through by
(1-\|\lambda^{(n)}\|_{\infty})
in the last inequality above, we deduce that (1)
\implies
(3).

Assume (2). Let
\|u(\lambda^{(n)})\|_{\mathcal{M}}\leq M
for some model
(\mathcal{M},u)
of
\varphi
and all
n\geq 1
and suppose that
\tau\in\mathbb{T}^{d}
. Clearly, for
j=1,\ldots,d
and for all
\lambda^{(n)}\in\mathbb{D}^{d}
,

\displaystyle\lvert\lambda^{(n)}_{j}\rvert<1

\displaystyle\iff 1+\lvert\lambda^{(n)}_{j}\rvert<2

\displaystyle\iff(1-\lvert\lambda^{(n)}_{j}\rvert)(1+\lvert\lambda^{(n)}_{j}\rvert)<2(1-\lvert\lambda^{(n)}_{j}\rvert)

\displaystyle\iff 1-\lvert\lambda^{(n)}_{j}\rvert^{2}<2(1-\lvert\lambda^{(n)}_{j}\rvert).

(4.1)

Note that

1-\lvert\lambda^{(n)}_{j}\rvert=\lvert\tau_{j}\rvert-\lvert\lambda^{(n)}_{j}\rvert\leq\lvert\tau_{j}-\lambda^{(n)}_{j}\rvert.

(4.2)

By equation (
2.6
),

\displaystyle 1-\lvert\varphi(\lambda^{(n)})\rvert

\displaystyle\leq 1-\lvert\varphi(\lambda^{(n)})\rvert^{2}

\displaystyle=\bigg\langle(1_{\mathcal{M}}-(\lambda^{(n)})_{P}^{*}(\lambda^{(n)})_{P})u(\lambda^{(n)}),u(\lambda^{(n)})\bigg\rangle_{\mathcal{M}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{d}(1-\lvert\lambda^{(n)}_{j}\rvert^{2})\|u_{j}(\lambda^{(n)})\|_{\mathcal{M}_{j}}^{2}.

By inequality (
4.1
),

1-\lvert\varphi(\lambda^{(n)})\rvert\leq\sum_{j=1}^{d}2(1-\lvert\lambda^{(n)}_{j}\rvert)\|u_{j}(\lambda^{(n)})\|_{\mathcal{M}_{j}}^{2}.

By inequality (
4.2
),

1-\lvert\varphi(\lambda^{(n)})\rvert\leq 2\sum_{j=1}^{d}\lvert\tau_{j}-\lambda^{(n)}_{j}\rvert\|u_{j}(\lambda^{(n)})\|_{\mathcal{M}_{j}}^{2}\leq 2\|\tau-\lambda^{(n)}\|_{\infty}\sum_{j=1}^{d}\|u_{j}(\lambda^{(n)})\|_{\mathcal{M}_{j}}^{2}.

By assumption,
\lambda^{(n)}{\overset{nt}{\rightarrow}}\tau
. Hence by inequality (
2.1
),

1-\lvert\varphi(\lambda^{(n)})\rvert\leq 2c(1-\|\lambda^{(n)}\|_{\infty})\sum_{j=1}^{d}\|u_{j}(\lambda^{(n)})\|_{\mathcal{M}_{j}}^{2}\\
\leq 2cM^{2}(1-\|\lambda^{(n)}\|_{\infty}).

Therefore (2)
\implies
(1) when
\tau\in\mathbb{T}^{d}
.

Let us prove that (2)
\implies
(1) when
\tau\in\partial\mathbb{D}^{d}\setminus\mathbb{T}^{d}
. Since any permutation of coordinates is an automorphism of
\overline{\mathbb{D}}^{d}
, it is enough to prove that (2)
\implies
(1) under the assumption that
\tau\in\mathbb{T}^{a}\times\mathbb{D}^{b}
for some positive integers
a,b
such that
a+b=d
. By Proposition
3.1
, there exists a
\omega\in\mathbb{T}
and
x\in\mathcal{M}
such that, for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{D}^{d}

1-\overline{\omega}\varphi(\lambda^{(n)})=\sum_{j=1}^{a}(1-\overline{\tau}_{j}\lambda^{(n)}_{j})\langle u_{j}(\lambda),x_{j}\rangle_{\mathcal{M}_{j}},

and therefore

\overline{\omega}\varphi(\lambda^{(n)})=1+\sum_{j=1}^{a}(\overline{\tau}_{j})(\lambda^{(n)}_{j}-\tau_{j})\langle u_{j}(\lambda^{(n)}),x_{j}\rangle_{\mathcal{M}_{j}}.

Thus,

\displaystyle\lvert\varphi(\lambda^{(n)})\rvert^{2}

\displaystyle=\bigg\lvert 1+\sum_{j=1}^{a}(\overline{\tau}_{j})(\lambda^{(n)}_{j}-\tau_{j})\langle u_{j}(\lambda^{(n)}),x_{j}\rangle_{\mathcal{M}_{j}}\bigg\rvert^{2}

\displaystyle=1+2\mathrm{Re}\bigg(\sum_{j=1}^{a}\overline{\tau}_{j}(\lambda^{(n)}_{j}-\tau_{j})\langle u_{j}(\lambda^{(n)}),x_{j}\rangle_{\mathcal{M}_{j}}\bigg)+\bigg\lvert\sum_{j=1}^{a}\overline{\tau}_{j}(\lambda^{(n)}_{j}-\tau_{j})\langle u_{j}(\lambda^{(n)}),x_{j}\rangle_{\mathcal{M}_{j}}\bigg\rvert^{2}.

Since
\lvert 1-\lvert\varphi(\lambda^{(n)})\rvert^{2}\rvert=1-\lvert\varphi(\lambda^{(n)})\rvert^{2}
, we get

\displaystyle 1-\lvert\varphi(\lambda^{(n)})\rvert^{2}

\displaystyle=\Bigg\lvert 2\mathrm{Re}\bigg(\sum_{j=1}^{a}\overline{\tau}_{j}(\lambda^{(n)}_{j}-\tau_{j})\langle u_{j}(\lambda^{(n)}),x_{j}\rangle_{\mathcal{M}_{j}}\bigg)+\bigg\lvert\sum_{j=1}^{a}\overline{\tau}_{j}(\lambda^{(n)}_{j}-\tau_{j})\langle u_{j}(\lambda^{(n)}),x_{j}\rangle_{\mathcal{M}_{j}}\bigg\rvert^{2}\Bigg\rvert.

Next, divide the above equation by
1-\|\lambda^{(n)}\|_{\infty}^{2}
to find the inequalities

\displaystyle\dfrac{1-\lvert\varphi(\lambda^{(n)})\rvert^{2}}{1-\|\lambda^{(n)}\|_{\infty}^{2}}

\displaystyle=\dfrac{1}{1-\|\lambda^{(n)}\|_{\infty}^{2}}\Bigg\lvert 2\mathrm{Re}\bigg(\sum_{j=1}^{a}\overline{\tau}_{j}(\lambda^{(n)}_{j}-\tau_{j})\langle u_{j}(\lambda^{(n)}),x_{j}\rangle_{\mathcal{M}_{j}}\bigg)

\displaystyle\hskip 184.9429pt+\bigg\lvert\sum_{j=1}^{a}\overline{\tau}_{j}(\lambda^{(n)}_{j}-\tau_{j})\langle u_{j}(\lambda^{(n)}),x_{j}\rangle_{\mathcal{M}_{j}}\bigg\rvert^{2}\Bigg\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\dfrac{1}{1-\|\lambda^{(n)}\|_{\infty}}\Bigg\lvert 2\mathrm{Re}\bigg(\sum_{j=1}^{a}\overline{\tau}_{j}(\lambda^{(n)}_{j}-\tau_{j})\langle u_{j}(\lambda^{(n)}),x_{j}\rangle_{\mathcal{M}_{j}}\bigg)

\displaystyle\hskip 184.9429pt+\bigg\lvert\sum_{j=1}^{a}\overline{\tau}_{j}(\lambda^{(n)}_{j}-\tau_{j})\langle u_{j}(\lambda^{(n)}),x_{j}\rangle_{\mathcal{M}_{j}}\bigg\rvert^{2}\Bigg\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\dfrac{1}{1-\|\lambda^{(n)}\|_{\infty}}\Bigg(2\bigg\lvert\sum_{j=1}^{a}\overline{\tau}_{j}(\lambda^{(n)}_{j}-\tau_{j})\langle u_{j}(\lambda^{(n)}),x_{j}\rangle_{\mathcal{M}_{j}}\bigg\rvert

\displaystyle\hskip 184.9429pt+\bigg\lvert\sum_{j=1}^{a}\overline{\tau}_{j}(\lambda^{(n)}_{j}-\tau_{j})\langle u_{j}(\lambda^{(n)}),x_{j}\rangle_{\mathcal{M}_{j}}\bigg\rvert^{2}\Bigg).

(4.3)

Define the vector
v:\mathbb{D}^{d}\rightarrow\mathcal{M}
by

v(\lambda)=\begin{bmatrix}\overline{\tau}_{1}(\lambda^{1}-\tau_{1})u_{1}(\lambda)\\
\vdots\\
\overline{\tau}_{a}(\lambda^{a}-\tau_{a})u_{a}(\lambda)\\
\end{bmatrix}~~\text{for all}~~\lambda\in\mathbb{D}^{d}.

Then the inequality (
4.3
) can be written in the following way

\displaystyle\dfrac{1-\lvert\varphi(\lambda^{(n)})\rvert^{2}}{1-\|\lambda^{(n)}\|_{\infty}^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\dfrac{1}{1-\|\lambda^{(n)}\|_{\infty}}\Bigg(2\Bigg\lvert\Bigg\langle v(\lambda^{(n)}),\begin{bmatrix}x_{1}\\
\vdots\\
x_{a}\\
\end{bmatrix}\Bigg\rangle_{\mathcal{M}}\Bigg\rvert+\Bigg\lvert\Bigg\langle v(\lambda^{(n)}),\begin{bmatrix}x_{1}\\
\vdots\\
x_{a}\\
\end{bmatrix}\Bigg\rangle_{\mathcal{M}}\Bigg\rvert^{2}\Bigg)

\displaystyle\leq\dfrac{1}{1-\|\lambda^{(n)}\|_{\infty}}\Bigg(2\|v(\lambda^{(n)})\|_{\mathcal{M}}\Bigg\|\begin{bmatrix}x_{1}\\
\vdots\\
x_{a}\\
\end{bmatrix}\Bigg\|_{\mathcal{M}}+\|v(\lambda^{(n)})\|_{\mathcal{M}}^{2}\Bigg\|\begin{bmatrix}x_{1}\\
\vdots\\
x_{a}\\
\end{bmatrix}\Bigg\|_{\mathcal{M}}^{2}\Bigg),

and so

\displaystyle\dfrac{1-\lvert\varphi(\lambda^{(n)})\rvert^{2}}{1-\|\lambda^{(n)}\|_{\infty}^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\dfrac{1}{1-\|\lambda^{(n)}\|_{\infty}}\Bigg(2\|\lambda^{(n)}-\tau\|_{\infty}\|u(\lambda^{(n)})\|_{\mathcal{M}}\|x\|_{\mathcal{M}}

\displaystyle\hskip 147.95424pt+\|x\|^{2}_{\mathcal{M}}\sum_{j=1}^{a}\lvert\lambda^{(n)}_{j}-\tau_{j}\rvert^{2}\|u_{j}(\lambda^{(n)})\|^{2}_{\mathcal{M}_{j}}\Bigg)

\displaystyle\leq\dfrac{1}{1-\|\lambda^{(n)}\|_{\infty}}\Bigg(2\|\lambda^{(n)}-\tau\|_{\infty}\|u(\lambda^{(n)})\|_{\mathcal{M}}\|x\|_{\mathcal{M}}

\displaystyle\hskip 147.95424pt+\|x\|^{2}_{\mathcal{M}}\|u(\lambda^{(n)})\|^{2}_{\mathcal{M}}\sum_{j=1}^{a}\lvert\lambda^{(n)}_{j}-\tau_{j}\rvert^{2}\Bigg).

By assumption (2),
\|u(\lambda^{(n)})\|_{\mathcal{M}}\leq M
. Therefore

\displaystyle\dfrac{1-\lvert\varphi(\lambda^{(n)})\rvert^{2}}{1-\|\lambda^{(n)}\|_{\infty}^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\dfrac{1}{1-\|\lambda^{(n)}\|_{\infty}}\Bigg(2M\|\lambda^{(n)}-\tau\|_{\infty}\|x\|_{\mathcal{M}}+M^{2}\|x\|^{2}_{\mathcal{M}}\sum_{j=1}^{a}\lvert\lambda^{(n)}_{j}-\tau_{j}\rvert\cdot\lvert\lambda^{(n)}_{j}-\tau_{j}\rvert\Bigg)

\displaystyle\leq 2M\|x\|_{\mathcal{M}}\dfrac{\|\lambda^{(n)}-\tau\|_{\infty}}{1-\|\lambda^{(n)}\|_{\infty}}+M^{2}\|x\|^{2}_{\mathcal{M}}\dfrac{\|\lambda^{(n)}-\tau\|_{\infty}}{1-\|\lambda^{(n)}\|_{\infty}}\sum_{j=1}^{a}\lvert\lambda^{(n)}_{j}-\tau_{j}\rvert.

Consider a set
S\subset\mathbb{D}^{d}
that approaches
\tau
in such a way that, for some
c\geq 1
,

\|\tau-\lambda\|_{\infty}\leq c(1-\|\lambda^{(n)}\|_{\infty})~~\text{for all}~n.

Thus, for
(\lambda^{(n)})
a sequence in
S
tending to
\tau
, we also have that, for large enough
n
,
\lvert\lambda^{(n)}_{j}-\tau_{j}\rvert<1
for all
j=1,\ldots,a
. Use both of these inequalities in the inequality above to see that if
(\lambda^{(n)})
is a sequence in
S
tending to
\tau
, for large enough
n
,

\dfrac{1-\lvert\varphi(\lambda^{(n)})\rvert^{2}}{1-\|\lambda^{(n)}\|_{\infty}^{2}}<2M\|x\|_{\mathcal{M}}c+M^{2}\|x\|^{2}_{\mathcal{M}}ca.

Thus, when
\tau\in\partial\mathbb{D}^{d}\setminus\mathbb{T}^{d}
, (2)
\implies
(1). Hence statements (1), (2) and (3) are equivalent.∎

The following statement gives us a bound on the model
(\mathcal{M},u)
of
\varphi\in\mathcal{SA}_{d}
in terms of a function-theoretic property of
\varphi
.

Proposition 4.2
.

Let
\varphi\in\mathcal{SA}_{d}
and let
(\mathcal{M},u)
be a model of
\varphi
. If
\tau\in\partial\mathbb{D}^{d}
is a
B
-point for
\varphi
then
u(\lambda)
is bounded on any set in
\mathbb{D}^{d}
that approaches
\tau
nontangentially with constant
c>0
such that

\|\lambda-\tau\|_{\infty}\leq c(1-\|\lambda\|_{\infty})

for all
\lambda\in S
. Moreover, if
S
approaches
\tau
nontangentially and
\alpha
is defined by

\liminf_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\rightarrow\tau\\
\lambda\in\mathbb{D}^{d}\end{subarray}}\dfrac{1-\lvert\varphi(\lambda)\rvert}{1-\|\lambda\|_{\infty}}=\alpha,

then

\|u(\lambda)\|\leq 2c\sqrt{\alpha}~~\text{for all}~\lambda\in S.

Proof.

For simplicity, we consider the case when
\tau\in\mathbb{T}^{a}\times\mathbb{D}^{b}
for some positive integers
a,b
such that
a+b=d
. The proof extends to all other permutations of the coordinates of
\tau\in\partial\mathbb{D}^{d}
. By Corollary
3.7
, there exists a
\omega\in\mathbb{T}
such that
\varphi(\lambda)\rightarrow\omega
as
\lambda{\overset{pnt}{\rightarrow}}\tau
. By Proposition
3.1
, there is an
x\in\mathcal{M}
such that
\|x\|_{\mathcal{M}}^{2}\leq\alpha
and the equation

1-\overline{\omega}\varphi(\lambda)=\sum_{\lvert\tau_{j}\rvert=1}\Big(1-\overline{\tau}_{j}\lambda_{j}\Big)\langle u_{j}(\lambda),x_{j}\rangle_{\mathcal{M}_{j}}

(4.4)

holds for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{D}^{d}
. Note in the equation above, by Proposition
3.1
, for
\tau\in\partial\mathbb{D}^{d}
such that
\lvert\tau_{j}\rvert<1
for some
j=1,\ldots,d
, we have
x_{j}=0
.
Fix a set
S
that approaches
\tau
nontangentially with the constant
c>0
. For
\lambda\in S
,

(1-\|\lambda\|_{\infty}^{2})\|u(\lambda)\|_{\mathcal{M}}^{2}=\sum_{j=1}^{d}(1-\|\lambda\|_{\infty}^{2})\|u_{j}(\lambda)\|_{\mathcal{M}_{j}}^{2}\leq\sum_{j=1}^{d}(1-\lvert\lambda_{j}\rvert^{2})\|u_{j}(\lambda)\|_{\mathcal{M}_{j}}^{2}.

By equation (
2.6
),

(1-\|\lambda\|_{\infty}^{2})\|u(\lambda)\|_{\mathcal{M}}^{2}=1-\lvert\varphi(\lambda)\rvert^{2}=1-\overline{\omega}\varphi(\lambda)+\overline{(\omega-\varphi(\lambda))}\varphi(\lambda).

By equation (
4.4
),

\displaystyle(1-\|\lambda\|_{\infty}^{2})\|u(\lambda)\|_{\mathcal{M}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2\lvert 1-\overline{\omega}\varphi(\lambda)\rvert

\displaystyle=2\bigg\lvert\sum_{\lvert\tau_{j}\rvert=1}\Big(1-\overline{\tau}_{j}\lambda_{j}\Big)\langle u_{j}(\lambda),x_{j}\rangle_{\mathcal{M}_{j}}\bigg\rvert

\displaystyle=2\Bigg\lvert\Bigg\langle\begin{bmatrix}(1-\overline{\tau_{1}}\lambda_{1})u_{1}(\lambda)\\
\vdots\\
(1-\overline{\tau_{a}}\lambda_{a})u_{a}(\lambda)\\
\end{bmatrix},\begin{bmatrix}x_{1}\\
\vdots\\
x_{a}\\
\end{bmatrix}\Bigg\rangle_{\mathcal{M}_{1}\oplus\ldots\oplus\mathcal{M}_{a}}\Bigg\rvert

\displaystyle\leq 2\Bigg\|\begin{bmatrix}(1-\overline{\tau_{1}}\lambda_{1})u_{1}(\lambda)\\
\vdots\\
(1-\overline{\tau_{a}}\lambda_{a})u_{a}(\lambda)\\
\end{bmatrix}\Bigg\|_{{}_{\mathcal{M}_{1}\oplus\ldots\oplus\mathcal{M}_{a}}}\Bigg\|\begin{bmatrix}x_{1}\\
\vdots\\
x_{a}\\
\end{bmatrix}\Bigg\|_{{}_{\mathcal{M}_{1}\oplus\ldots\oplus\mathcal{M}_{a}}}

\displaystyle\leq 2\|\tau-\lambda\|_{\infty}\|u(\lambda)\|_{\mathcal{M}}\|x\|_{\mathcal{M}}.

By equation (
2.1
),

(1-\|\lambda\|_{\infty}^{2})\|u(\lambda)\|_{\mathcal{M}}^{2}\leq 2c(1-\|\lambda\|_{\infty})\|u(\lambda)\|_{\mathcal{M}}\sqrt{\alpha}.

For
\lambda\in S
,
\|\lambda\|_{\infty}\leq 1
and
\|\lambda\|_{\infty}^{2}\leq\|\lambda\|_{\infty}
. Thus
1-\|\lambda\|_{\infty}\leq 1-\|\lambda\|_{\infty}^{2}
, and so the above calculations show that

(1-\|\lambda\|_{\infty}^{2})\|u(\lambda)\|_{\mathcal{M}}^{2}\leq 2c(1-\|\lambda\|_{\infty}^{2})\|u(\lambda)\|_{\mathcal{M}}\sqrt{\alpha}.

Hence for
\lambda\in S
,
\|u(\lambda)\|\leq 2c\sqrt{\alpha}
.∎

All of the results up till now will be used to prove the following corollary and proposition.

Corollary 4.3
.

Let
\varphi\in\mathcal{SA}_{d}
, let
\tau\in\partial\mathbb{D}^{d}
and let
(\mathcal{M},u)
be a model of
\varphi
. The following conditions are equivalent:

(1)

\dfrac{1-\lvert\varphi(\lambda)\rvert}{1-\|\lambda\|_{\infty}}
is bounded on some sequence
\{\lambda^{(n)}\}
that converges to
\tau
;

(2)

u(\lambda^{(n)})
is bounded on some sequence
\{\lambda^{(n)}\}
that approaches
\tau
nontangentially;

(3)

\dfrac{1-\lvert\varphi(\lambda)\rvert}{1-\|\lambda\|_{\infty}}
is bounded on every subset of
\mathbb{D}^{d}
that approaches
\tau
nontangentially;

(4)

u(\lambda)
is bounded on every subset of
\mathbb{D}^{d}
that approaches
\tau
nontangentially.

Proof.

(1)
\iff
(2) and (3)
\iff
(4) are equivalent by Propositions
4.1
and
3.5
respectively. (3)
\implies
(1) and (4)
\implies
(2) trivially. Finally, (1) is the definition that
\tau
is a
B
-point for
\varphi
, so that (1)
\implies
(4) is just a restatement of Proposition
4.2
.∎

Proposition 4.4
.

Let
\varphi\in\mathcal{SA}_{d}
, let
(\mathcal{M},u)
be a model of
\varphi
and let
\tau\in\partial\mathbb{D}^{d}
be a
B
-point for
\varphi
. Suppose that
\{\lambda^{(n)}\}
converges to
\tau
nontangentially in
\mathbb{D}^{d}
. If
\{u(\lambda^{(n)})\}
converges weakly in
\mathcal{M}
as
n\rightarrow\infty
, then
\{u(\lambda^{(n)})\}
converges in norm as
n\rightarrow\infty
.

Proof.

Fix a sequence
\{\lambda^{(n)}\}
in
\mathbb{D}^{d}
such that
\lambda^{(n)}{\overset{nt}{\rightarrow}}\tau
and
u(\lambda^{(n)})\rightarrow x
weakly in
\mathcal{M}
. By Corollary
3.7
, there exists
\omega\in\mathbb{T}
such that
\varphi(\lambda)\rightarrow\omega~~\text{as}~~\lambda{\overset{pnt}{\rightarrow}}\tau.
By Proposition
3.1
,

1-\overline{\omega}\varphi(\lambda)=\sum_{\lvert\tau_{j}\rvert=1}\Big(1-\overline{\tau}_{j}\lambda_{j}\Big)\langle u_{j}(\lambda),x_{j}\rangle_{\mathcal{M}_{j}}

holds for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{D}^{d}
. The equality

\|u(\lambda^{(n)})-x\|_{\mathcal{M}}^{2}=\langle u(\lambda^{(n)})-x,u(\lambda^{(n)})-x\rangle_{\mathcal{M}}

implies that

\displaystyle\|u(\lambda^{(n)})-x\|_{\mathcal{M}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\|u(\lambda^{(n)})\|_{\mathcal{M}}^{2}-2\mathrm{Re}\Big(\langle u(\lambda^{(n)}),x\rangle_{\mathcal{M}}\Big)+\|x\|_{\mathcal{M}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\bigg(\|u(\lambda^{(n)})\|_{\mathcal{M}}^{2}-\mathrm{Re}\Big(\langle u(\lambda^{(n)}),x\rangle_{\mathcal{M}}\Big)\bigg)

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt+\bigg(\|x\|_{\mathcal{M}}^{2}-\mathrm{Re}\Big(\langle u(\lambda^{(n)}),x\rangle_{\mathcal{M}}\Big)\bigg).

(4.5)

Since
u(\lambda^{(n)})\rightarrow x
weakly as
n\rightarrow\infty
,

\langle u(\lambda^{(n)}),x\rangle_{\mathcal{M}}\rightarrow\langle x,x\rangle_{\mathcal{M}}~~\text{as}~n\rightarrow\infty.

Thus

u(\lambda^{(n)})\rightarrow x~\text{weakly in}~\mathcal{M}\implies\|x\|_{\mathcal{M}}^{2}-\mathrm{Re}\big(\langle u(\lambda^{(n)}),x\rangle_{\mathcal{M}}\big)\rightarrow 0~\text{as}~n\rightarrow\infty.

(4.6)

To show that
u(\lambda^{(n)})
converges in norm to
x
as
n\rightarrow\infty
, all that remains is to show that the first term on the right hand side of equation (
4.5
) also tends to zero.

By Proposition
3.1
, if
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{d})=x\in Y_{\tau}
where
\tau\in\partial\mathbb{D}^{d}
is such that
(\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{d})=\tau\in\mathbb{T}^{a}\times\mathbb{D}^{b}
where
a+b=d
, then for each
\tau_{j}\in\mathbb{D}
,
x_{j}=0
. By Proposition
3.1
, there exists a
\omega\in\mathbb{T}
such that for all
x\in Y_{\tau}
and
\lambda\in\mathbb{D}^{d}
,

1-\overline{\omega}\varphi(\lambda^{(n)})=\sum_{j=1}^{d}\Big(1-\overline{\tau}_{j}\lambda^{(n)}_{j}\Big)\langle u_{j}(\lambda^{(n)}),x_{j}\rangle_{\mathcal{M}_{j}}.

(4.7)

By equations (
2.7
) and (
4.7
),

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{d}(1-\lvert\lambda_{j}^{(n)}\rvert^{2})\|u_{j}(\lambda^{(n)})\|_{\mathcal{M}_{j}}^{2}

\displaystyle=1-\lvert\varphi(\lambda^{(n)})\rvert^{2}

\displaystyle=1-\lvert\varphi(\lambda^{(n)})\rvert^{2}-2\mathrm{Re}\big(1-\overline{\omega}\varphi(\lambda^{(n)})\big)

\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102pt+\mathrm{Re}\bigg(\sum_{j=1}^{d}2\Big(1-\overline{\tau}_{j}\lambda^{(n)}_{j}\Big)\langle u_{j}(\lambda^{(n)}),x_{j}\rangle_{\mathcal{M}_{j}}\bigg).

(4.8)

Subtraction of
\sum_{j=1}^{d}(1-\lvert\lambda^{(n)}_{j}\rvert^{2})\mathrm{Re}\Big(\langle u_{j}(\lambda^{(n)}),x_{j}\rangle_{\mathcal{M}_{j}}\Big)
from both sides of equation (
4.8
) gives us

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{d}(1-\lvert\lambda^{(n)}_{j}\rvert^{2})\Big(\|u_{j}(\lambda^{(n)})\|_{\mathcal{M}_{j}}^{2}-\mathrm{Re}(\langle u_{j}(\lambda^{(n)}),x_{j}\rangle)\Big)

\displaystyle=1-\lvert\varphi(\lambda^{(n)})\rvert^{2}-2\mathrm{Re}\big(1-\overline{\omega}\varphi(\lambda^{(n)})\big)

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt+\mathrm{Re}\bigg[\sum_{j=1}^{d}\Big(2(1-\overline{\tau}_{j}\lambda^{(n)}_{j})-(1-\lvert\lambda^{(n)}_{j}\rvert^{2})\Big)\langle u_{j}(\lambda^{(n)}),x_{j}\rangle_{\mathcal{M}_{j}}\bigg]

\displaystyle=-1+2\mathrm{Re}\big(\overline{\omega}\varphi(\lambda^{(n)})\big)-\lvert\varphi(\lambda^{(n)})\rvert^{2}

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt+\mathrm{Re}\bigg[\sum_{j=1}^{d}\Big(1-2\overline{\tau}_{j}\lambda^{(n)}_{j}+\lvert\lambda^{(n)}_{j}\rvert^{2}\Big)\langle u_{j}(\lambda^{(n)}),x_{j}\rangle_{\mathcal{M}_{j}}\bigg]

\displaystyle=-\lvert 1-\overline{\omega}\varphi(\lambda^{(n)})\rvert^{2}+\mathrm{Re}\bigg[\sum_{j=1}^{d}\Big(1-2\overline{\tau}_{j}\lambda^{(n)}_{j}+\lvert\lambda^{(n)}_{j}\rvert^{2}\Big)\langle u_{j}(\lambda^{(n)}),x_{j}\rangle_{\mathcal{M}_{j}}\bigg]

\displaystyle=-\lvert 1-\overline{\omega}\varphi(\lambda^{(n)})\rvert^{2}+\mathrm{Re}\Big[\sum_{j=1}^{d}\Big(1-2\overline{\tau}_{j}\lambda^{(n)}_{j}+\lvert\lambda^{(n)}_{j}\rvert^{2}\Big)\|x_{j}\|_{\mathcal{M}_{j}}^{2}\Big]

\displaystyle\hskip 71.13188pt+\mathrm{Re}\bigg[\sum_{j=1}^{d}\Big(1-2\overline{\tau}_{j}\lambda^{(n)}_{j}+\lvert\lambda^{(n)}_{j}\rvert^{2}\Big)\langle u_{j}(\lambda^{(n)})-x_{j},x_{j}\rangle_{\mathcal{M}_{j}}\bigg].

(4.9)

Note that for
\lambda\in\mathbb{D}^{d}
,
\lvert\lambda_{j}\rvert^{2}\leq\lvert\lambda_{j}\rvert\leq\|\lambda\|_{\infty}
for
j=1,\ldots,d
, and so

\dfrac{1}{1-\lvert\lambda_{j}\rvert^{2}}\leq\dfrac{1}{1-\|\lambda\|_{\infty}}.

By assumption,
\lambda^{(n)}{\overset{nt}{\rightarrow}}\tau
as
n\rightarrow\infty
. Hence there exists a
c>0
such that

\|\lambda^{(n)}-\tau\|_{\infty}\leq c(1-\|\lambda^{(n)}\|_{\infty}).

Consequently,

\dfrac{\|\lambda^{(n)}-\tau\|_{\infty}}{1-\lvert\lambda^{(n)}_{j}\rvert^{2}}\leq\dfrac{\|\lambda^{(n)}-\tau\|_{\infty}}{1-\|\lambda^{(n)}\|_{\infty}}\leq c,

for some
c>0
, so that

\dfrac{1-\lvert\lambda^{(n)}_{j}\rvert^{2}}{\|\lambda^{(n)}-\tau\|_{\infty}}\geq\dfrac{1}{c}.

(4.10)

It is easy to see that, for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{D}^{d}
and
\tau\in\partial\mathbb{D}^{d}
,

1-\|\lambda\|_{\infty}\leq\|\lambda-\tau\|_{\infty}

and, for
j=1,\ldots,d
,

1-\lvert\lambda_{j}\rvert^{2}\leq\|\tau+\lambda\|_{\infty}\|\tau-\lambda\|_{\infty}.

Let
c_{1}=\max_{i=1,\ldots d}\lvert\tau_{i}+\lambda_{i}\rvert=\|\tau+\lambda\|_{\infty}
. Therefore we have the inequality

1-\lvert\lambda^{(n)}_{j}\rvert^{2}\leq c_{1}\|\tau-\lambda^{(n)}\|_{\infty}.

Equivalently, for
j=1,\ldots,d
,

\dfrac{1}{\|\tau-\lambda^{(n)}\|_{\infty}}\leq\dfrac{c_{1}}{1-\lvert\lambda^{(n)}_{j}\rvert^{2}}.

(4.11)

Observe the following two cases. Assume firstly that
\tau\in\mathbb{T}^{d}
. By inequality (
4.10
),

\displaystyle\dfrac{1}{\|\lambda^{(n)}-\tau\|_{\infty}}\sum_{j=1}^{d}(1-\lvert\lambda^{(n)}_{j}\rvert^{2})\Big(\|u_{j}(\lambda^{(n)})\|_{\mathcal{M}_{j}}^{2}-\mathrm{Re}(\langle u_{j}(\lambda^{(n)}),x_{j}\rangle_{\mathcal{M}_{j}})\Big)

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{j=1}^{d}\Big(\dfrac{1}{c}\Big)\Big(\|u_{j}(\lambda^{(n)})\|_{\mathcal{M}_{j}}^{2}-\mathrm{Re}(\langle u_{j}(\lambda^{(n)}),x_{j}\rangle_{\mathcal{M}_{j}})\Big)

\displaystyle=\dfrac{1}{c}\cdot\Big(\|u(\lambda^{(n)})\|^{2}_{\mathcal{M}}-\mathrm{Re}(\langle u(\lambda^{(n)}),x\rangle_{\mathcal{M}})\Big).

(4.12)

Then, by equations (
4.9
) and (
4.12
),

\displaystyle\|u(\lambda^{(n)})\|^{2}_{\mathcal{M}}-\mathrm{Re}(\langle u(\lambda^{(n)}),x\rangle)

\displaystyle\leq\dfrac{c}{\|\lambda^{(n)}-\tau\|_{\infty}}\cdot\sum_{j=1}^{d}(1-\lvert\lambda^{(n)}_{j}\rvert^{2})\Big(\|u_{j}(\lambda^{(n)})\|_{\mathcal{M}_{j}}^{2}-\mathrm{Re}(\langle u_{j}(\lambda^{(n)}),x_{j}\rangle_{\mathcal{M}_{j}})\Big)

\displaystyle=\Bigg(\dfrac{c}{\|\lambda^{(n)}-\tau\|_{\infty}}\Bigg)\Bigg(-\lvert 1-\overline{\omega}\varphi(\lambda^{(n)})\rvert^{2}+\sum_{j=1}^{d}\mathrm{Re}\Big(1-2\overline{\tau}_{j}\lambda^{(n)}_{j}+\lvert\lambda^{(n)}_{j}\rvert^{2}\Big)\|x_{j}\|_{\mathcal{M}_{j}}^{2}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+\mathrm{Re}\bigg[\sum_{j=1}^{d}\Big(1-2\overline{\tau}_{j}\lambda^{(n)}_{j}+\lvert\lambda^{(n)}_{j}\rvert^{2}\Big)\langle u_{j}(\lambda^{(n)})-x_{j},x_{j}\rangle_{\mathcal{M}_{j}}\bigg]\Bigg)

(4.13)

\displaystyle=c\Bigg(\dfrac{-\lvert 1-\overline{\omega}\varphi(\lambda^{(n)})\rvert^{2}}{\|\lambda^{(n)}-\tau\|_{\infty}}+\sum_{j=1}^{d}\mathrm{Re}\Big(1-2\overline{\tau}_{j}\lambda^{(n)}_{j}+\lvert\lambda^{(n)}_{j}\rvert^{2}\Big)\dfrac{\|x_{j}\|_{\mathcal{M}_{j}}^{2}}{\|\lambda^{(n)}-\tau\|_{\infty}}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+\sum_{j=1}^{d}\dfrac{1}{\|\lambda^{(n)}-\tau\|_{\infty}}\mathrm{Re}\bigg[\Big(1-2\overline{\tau}_{j}\lambda^{(n)}_{j}+\lvert\lambda^{(n)}_{j}\rvert^{2}\Big)\langle u_{j}(\lambda^{(n)})-x_{j},x_{j}\rangle_{\mathcal{M}_{j}}\bigg]\Bigg).

By equations (
4.11
) and (
4.13
),

\displaystyle\|u(\lambda^{(n)})\|_{\mathcal{M}}^{2}-\mathrm{Re}(\langle u(\lambda^{(n)}),x\rangle_{\mathcal{M}})

\displaystyle\leq c\Bigg(\dfrac{-\lvert 1-\overline{\omega}\varphi(\lambda^{(n)})\rvert^{2}}{1-\|\lambda^{(n)}\|_{\infty}}+c_{1}\sum_{j=1}^{d}\dfrac{\mathrm{Re}\Big[\Big(1-2\overline{\tau}_{j}\lambda^{(n)}_{j}+\lvert\lambda^{(n)}_{j}\rvert^{2}\Big)\|x_{j}\|_{\mathcal{M}_{j}}^{2}\Big)\Big]}{1-\lvert\lambda^{(n)}_{j}\rvert^{2}}

\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102pt+c_{1}\sum_{j=1}^{d}\dfrac{\mathrm{Re}\bigg[\Big(1-2\overline{\tau}_{j}\lambda^{(n)}_{j}+\lvert\lambda^{(n)}_{j}\rvert^{2}\Big)\langle u_{j}(\lambda^{(n)})-x_{j},x_{j}\rangle_{\mathcal{M}_{j}}\bigg]}{1-\lvert\lambda^{(n)}_{j}\rvert^{2}}\Bigg)

\displaystyle\leq c\Bigg(\dfrac{-\lvert 1-\overline{\omega}\varphi(\lambda^{(n)})\rvert^{2}}{1-\|\lambda^{(n)}\|_{\infty}}

\displaystyle\hskip 51.21504pt+c_{1}\|x\|_{\mathcal{M}}^{2}\sum_{j=1}^{d}\dfrac{\lvert 1-\overline{\tau_{j}}\lambda^{(n)}_{j}\rvert^{2}}{1-\lvert\lambda^{(n)}_{j}\rvert^{2}}+c_{1}\sum_{j=1}^{d}\dfrac{\lvert 1-\overline{\tau_{j}}\lambda^{(n)}_{j}\rvert^{2}\lvert\langle u_{j}(\lambda^{(n)})-x_{j},x_{j}\rangle_{\mathcal{M}_{j}}\rvert}{1-\lvert\lambda^{(n)}_{j}\rvert^{2}}\Bigg)

\displaystyle\leq c\Bigg(\dfrac{-\lvert 1-\overline{\omega}\varphi(\lambda^{(n)})\rvert^{2}}{1-\|\lambda^{(n)}\|_{\infty}}

\displaystyle\hskip 22.76228pt+c_{1}\|x\|_{\mathcal{M}}^{2}\sum_{j=1}^{d}\dfrac{\lvert\tau_{j}-\lambda^{(n)}_{j}\rvert^{2}}{1-\lvert\lambda^{(n)}_{j}\rvert^{2}}+c_{1}\sum_{j=1}^{d}\dfrac{\lvert\tau_{j}-\lambda^{(n)}_{j}\rvert(1+\lvert\lambda^{(n)}_{j}\rvert)}{1-\lvert\lambda^{(n)}_{j}\rvert^{2}}\lvert\langle u_{j}(\lambda^{(n)})-x_{j},x_{j}\rangle_{\mathcal{M}}\rvert\Bigg).

By Corollary
4.3
, the first term on the right tends to zero since
\varphi(\lambda^{(n)})\rightarrow\omega
, the second term tends to zero because
\lambda^{(n)}{\overset{nt}{\rightarrow}}\tau
and the third term ends to zero since
u(\lambda^{(n)})\rightarrow x
weakly in
\mathcal{M}
. Therefore
u(\lambda)
converges in norm to
x
when
\tau\in\mathbb{T}^{d}
.

For the second case, let
a+b=d
such that
\tau\in\mathbb{T}^{a}\times\mathbb{D}^{b}
. By Proposition
3.1
, we have for
j=b,\ldots,d
,
x_{j}=0
. Indeed, as

1-\lvert\varphi(\lambda)\rvert^{2}=\sum_{j=1}^{d}\big(1-\lvert\lambda_{j}\rvert^{2}\big)\|u_{j}(\lambda)\|_{\mathcal{M}_{j}}^{2},

then
\|u_{j}(\lambda^{(n)})\|_{\mathcal{M}_{j}}
tends to zero for all
j=b,\ldots,d
. Therefore, for
\tau=\mathbb{T}^{a}\times\mathbb{D}^{b}
, if we simply repeat the calculations as before in the case for
\tau\in\mathbb{T}^{d}
, we produce the same result, that is, for
\tau\in\mathbb{T}^{a}\times\mathbb{D}^{b}
, for each
j=1,\ldots,a
,

\|u_{j}(\lambda^{(n)})-x_{j}\|_{\mathcal{M}_{j}}\rightarrow 0~~\text{as}~~\lambda^{(n)}{\overset{nt}{\rightarrow}}\tau.

∎

Theorem 4.5
.

Let
\varphi\in\mathcal{SA}_{d}
, let
\tau\in\partial\mathbb{D}^{d}
be a
B
-point for
\varphi
and let
(\mathcal{M},u)
be a model of
\varphi
. For any realization
(a,\beta,\gamma,D)
of
(\mathcal{M},u)
there exists a unique vector
u(\tau)\in\mathcal{M}
such that
(1_{\mathcal{M}}-D\tau_{P})u(\tau)=\gamma
and
u(\tau)\perp\mathrm{Ker}(1_{\mathcal{M}}-D\tau_{P})
where
P
is the
d
-tuple
P=(P_{1},\ldots,P_{d})
on
\mathcal{M}
such that
P_{j}:\mathcal{M}\rightarrow\mathcal{M}
is the orthogonal projection onto
\mathcal{M}_{j}
for
j=1,\ldots,d
. Furthermore, if
S\subset\mathbb{D}
approaches
1
nontangentially, then

\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}z\rightarrow 1\\
z\in S\end{subarray}}u(z\tau)=u(\tau).

(4.14)

Consequently,

\|u(\tau)\|_{\mathcal{M}}^{2}=\liminf_{\lambda\rightarrow\tau}\dfrac{1-\lvert\varphi(\lambda)\rvert^{2}}{1-\|\lambda\|^{2}_{\infty}}.

(4.15)

Proof.

Consider the sequence
\lambda^{(n)}=(1-1/n)\tau
in
\mathbb{D}^{d}
for
