1-\overline{\varphi(\mu)}\varphi(\lambda)=\Big\langle\big(1_{\mathcal{M}}-I(\mu)^{*}I(\lambda)\big)u({\lambda}),u({\mu})\Big\rangle_{\mathcal{M}};

(8.17)

(2)

\tau
is a
C
-point of the model
(\mathcal{M},u,I)
;

(3)

\tau
is a carapoint for
I
and
\lim_{\lambda{\overset{nt}{\rightarrow}}\tau}I(\lambda)=1_{\mathcal{M}}
.

Since
\tau
is a
C
-point of the model
(\mathcal{M},u,I)
, there exists a vector
u(\tau)\in\mathcal{M}
such that

\lim_{\lambda{\overset{nt}{\rightarrow}}\tau}u(\lambda)=u(\tau).

(8.18)

Fix
\mu\in\mathbb{D}^{d}
such that
\varphi(\mu)\neq 0
. Consider a set
S\subset\mathbb{D}^{d}
such that
S{\overset{nt}{\rightarrow}}\tau
.
Then there exists a non-tangential limit of the right side of equation (
8.17
):

\lim_{\lambda{\rightarrow}\tau,\lambda\in S}\Big\langle\big(1_{\mathcal{M}}-I(\mu)^{*}I(\lambda)\big)u({\lambda}),u({\mu})\Big\rangle_{\mathcal{M}}=\Big\langle\big(1_{\mathcal{M}}-I(\mu)^{*}\big)u({\tau}),u({\mu})\Big\rangle_{\mathcal{M}}.

(8.19)

Hence, by equations (
8.17
) and (
8.19
), there is a non-tangential limit

\lim_{\lambda{\rightarrow}\tau,\lambda\in S}\overline{\varphi(\mu)}\varphi(\lambda)=1-\Big\langle u({\tau}),u({\mu})\Big\rangle_{\mathcal{M}}+\Big\langle I(\mu)^{*}u({\tau}),u({\mu})\Big\rangle_{\mathcal{M}}.

(8.20)

Therefore,
\lim_{\lambda{\overset{nt}{\rightarrow}}\tau}\varphi(\lambda)
exists. Let its value be
\omega
.

Let us show that
|\omega|=1
. Let
\lambda=\mu
in equation (
8.17
), to obtain

1-|\varphi(\lambda)|^{2}=\Big\langle\big(1_{\mathcal{M}}-I(\lambda)^{*}I(\lambda)\big)u({\lambda}),u({\lambda})\Big\rangle_{\mathcal{M}}.

(8.21)

Note that, since
\lim_{\lambda{\overset{nt}{\rightarrow}}\tau}I(\lambda)=1_{\mathcal{M}}
, if
S{\overset{nt}{\rightarrow}}\tau
then

\lim_{\lambda{\rightarrow}\tau,\lambda\in S}\Big\langle\big(1_{\mathcal{M}}-I(\lambda)^{*}I(\lambda)\big)u({\lambda}),u({\lambda})\Big\rangle_{\mathcal{M}}=0.

Hence, by equation (
8.21
),

\lim_{\lambda{\overset{nt}{\rightarrow}}\tau}(1-|\varphi(\lambda)|^{2})=0
.
Since
\omega=\lim_{\lambda{\overset{nt}{\rightarrow}}\tau}\varphi(\lambda)
, it follows that
|\omega|=1
.
∎

Corollary 8.4
.

If
\tau\in\mathbb{T}^{d}
is a carapoint for a function
\varphi\in\mathcal{SA}_{d}
, then
\varphi
has a generalized realization

\varphi(\lambda)=a+\langle I(\lambda)(1_{\mathcal{M}}-QI(\lambda))^{-1}\gamma,{\hat{\beta}}\rangle_{\mathcal{M}}~~\text{for all}~~\lambda\in\mathbb{D}^{d},

(8.22)

for some Hilbert space
\mathcal{M}
, a scalar
a\in\mathbb{C}
, vectors
{\hat{\beta}},\gamma\in\mathcal{M}
, a contraction
Q
on
\mathcal{M}
satisfying
\mathrm{Ker}(1_{\mathcal{M}}-Q)=\{0\}
and an operator-valued analytic function
I:\mathbb{D}^{d}\rightarrow\mathcal{B(M)}
such that
I
is inner and

I(\lambda)=1_{\mathcal{M}}-\bigg(\dfrac{\mathds{1}}{\mathds{1}-\overline{\tau}\lambda}\bigg)^{-1}_{Y},

(8.23)

for some
d
-tuple of positive contractions
Y=(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{d})
on
\mathcal{M}
for which
\sum_{j=1}^{d}Y_{j}=1_{\mathcal{M}}
.

Proof.

In the proof of Theorem
8.2
we chose a model
(\mathcal{L},v)
for
\varphi\in\mathcal{SA}_{d}
and any realization
(a,\beta,\gamma,D)
of
(\mathcal{L},v)
such that

\varphi(\lambda)=a+\langle\lambda_{P}v(\lambda),\beta\rangle_{\mathcal{L}},

(8.24)

for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{D}^{d}
where
\mathcal{L}
is a separable Hilbert space with orthogonal decomposition
\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{L}_{1}\oplus\ldots\oplus\mathcal{L}_{d}
and
\lambda_{P}=\lambda_{1}P_{1}+\ldots\lambda_{d}P_{d}
such that
P=(P_{1},\ldots,P_{d})
is the
d
-tuple of operators on
\mathcal{L}
where
P_{j}:\mathcal{L}\rightarrow\mathcal{L}
is the orthogonal projection onto
\mathcal{L}_{j}
for
j=1,\ldots,d
.

We defined
\mathcal{N}=\mathrm{Ker}(1_{\mathcal{L}}-D\tau_{P})
and wrote
D\tau_{P}
in the block matrix form relative to the orthogonal decomposition
\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{N}\oplus\mathcal{N}^{\perp}
,

D\tau_{P}=\begin{bmatrix}1_{\mathcal{N}}&0\\
0&Q\end{bmatrix}.

(8.25)

It is clear from the definition of
Q
that
Q
is a contraction and
\mathrm{Ker}(1_{\mathcal{N}^{\perp}}-Q)=\{0\}
.

Moreover, we proved that there exists a generalized model
(\mathcal{N}^{\perp},u,I)
of
\varphi
, that is,
\mathcal{N}^{\perp}
is a separable Hilbert space,
u:\mathbb{D}^{d}\rightarrow\mathcal{N}^{\perp}
is an analytic mapping such that
u(\lambda)=P_{\mathcal{N}^{\perp}}v(\lambda)
for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{D}^{d}
,
I
is a contractive, analytic
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{N}^{\perp})
-valued function on
\mathbb{D}^{d}
such that
\|I(\lambda)\|_{\mathcal{N}^{\perp}}<1
for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{D}^{d}
,
I
is inner and
I
is defined by equation (
8.23
). Thus, for all
\lambda,\mu\in\mathbb{D}^{d}
,

1-\overline{\varphi(\mu)}\varphi(\lambda)=\langle(1_{\mathcal{N}^{\perp}}-I^{*}(\mu)I(\lambda))u(\lambda),u(\mu)\rangle_{\mathcal{N}^{\perp}}.

By equation (
8.11
),

\gamma=(1_{\mathcal{N}^{\perp}}-QI(\lambda))u(\lambda).

(8.26)

By Lemma
5.2
,
\overline{\tau}_{P}\beta\in\mathcal{N}^{\perp}
. Define
{\hat{\beta}}=\overline{\tau}_{P}\beta
. Therefore by equations (
8.24
) and (
8.26
),

\displaystyle\varphi(\lambda)

\displaystyle=a+\langle\lambda_{P}v(\lambda),\beta\rangle_{\mathcal{L}}

\displaystyle=a+\langle\overline{\tau}_{P}\lambda_{P}v(\lambda),\overline{\tau}_{P}\beta\rangle_{\mathcal{L}}

\displaystyle=a+\langle P_{\mathcal{N}^{\perp}}\overline{\tau}_{P}\lambda_{P}v(\lambda),\overline{\tau}_{P}\beta\rangle_{\mathcal{N}^{\perp}}

\displaystyle=a+\langle I(\lambda)u(\lambda),\overline{\tau}_{P}\beta\rangle_{\mathcal{N}^{\perp}}

\displaystyle=a+\langle I(\lambda)u(\lambda),{\tilde{\beta}}\rangle_{\mathcal{N}^{\perp}}.

Note that

\begin{bmatrix}a&1\otimes{\hat{\beta}}\\
\gamma\otimes 1&Q\\
\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
I(\lambda)u(\lambda)\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}a+\langle I(\lambda)u(\lambda),{\hat{\beta}}\rangle_{\mathcal{N}^{\perp}}\\
\gamma+QI(\lambda)u(\lambda)\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\varphi(\lambda)\\
u(\lambda)\end{bmatrix}.

We show that the operator

\begin{bmatrix}a&1\otimes{\hat{\beta}}\\
\gamma\otimes 1&Q\\
\end{bmatrix}

is a unitary operator on
\mathbb{C}\oplus\mathcal{N}^{\perp}
. Indeed, define
P_{\mathbb{C}\oplus\{0\}\oplus\mathcal{N}^{\perp}}
and
P_{\mathbb{C}\oplus\mathcal{N}^{\perp}}
to be the orthogonal projections on
\mathbb{C}\oplus\mathcal{L}
such that

P_{\mathbb{C}\oplus\{0\}\oplus\mathcal{N}^{\perp}}=\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
0&0\\
0&1_{\mathcal{N}^{\perp}}\\
\end{bmatrix},~P_{\mathbb{C}\oplus\mathcal{N}^{\perp}}=\begin{bmatrix}1&0&0\\
0&0&1_{\mathcal{N}^{\perp}}\\
\end{bmatrix},

where

P^{*}_{\mathbb{C}\oplus\{0\}\oplus\mathcal{N}^{\perp}}P_{\mathbb{C}\oplus\{0\}\oplus\mathcal{N}^{\perp}}=1_{\mathbb{C}\oplus\mathcal{N}^{\perp}}~\text{and}~~P^{*}_{\mathbb{C}\oplus\mathcal{N}^{\perp}}P_{\mathbb{C}\oplus\mathcal{N}^{\perp}}=1_{\mathbb{C}\oplus\mathcal{L}}.

(8.27)

By Lemma
5.2
,
\gamma\in\mathcal{N}^{\perp}
. Therefore the unitary operator

L=\begin{bmatrix}a&1\otimes\beta\\
\gamma\otimes 1&D\end{bmatrix}

(8.28)

on
\mathbb{C}\oplus\mathcal{L}
such that
\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{N}\oplus\mathcal{N}^{\perp}
can be written as

L=\begin{bmatrix}a&1\otimes\beta|_{\mathcal{N}}&1\otimes\beta|_{\mathcal{N}^{\perp}}\\
0&D_{11}&D_{12}\\
\gamma\otimes 1&D_{21}&D_{22}\end{bmatrix}.

Thus

\displaystyle P_{\mathbb{C}\oplus\mathcal{N}^{\perp}}\begin{bmatrix}a&1\otimes\beta|_{\mathcal{N}}&1\otimes\beta|_{\mathcal{N}^{\perp}}\\
0&D_{11}&D_{12}\\
\gamma\otimes 1&D_{21}&D_{22}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1&0&0\\
0&\tau_{P}|_{\mathcal{N}}&0\\
0&0&\tau_{P}|_{\mathcal{N}^{\perp}}\end{bmatrix}P_{\mathbb{C}\oplus\{0\}\oplus\mathcal{N}^{\perp}}

\displaystyle=P_{\mathbb{C}\oplus\mathcal{N}^{\perp}}\begin{bmatrix}a&\tau_{P}\otimes\beta|_{\mathcal{N}}&\tau_{P}\otimes\beta|_{\mathcal{N}^{\perp}}\\
0&D_{11}\tau_{P}&D_{12}\tau_{P}\\
\gamma\otimes 1&D_{21}\tau_{P}&D_{22}\tau_{P}\end{bmatrix}P_{\mathbb{C}\oplus\{0\}\oplus\mathcal{N}^{\perp}}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}1&0&0\\
0&0&1_{\mathcal{N}^{\perp}}\\
\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}a&1\otimes\overline{\tau}_{P}\beta|_{\mathcal{N}}&1\otimes\overline{\tau}_{P}\beta|_{\mathcal{N}^{\perp}}\\
0&D_{11}\tau_{P}&D_{12}\tau_{P}\\
\gamma\otimes 1&D_{21}\tau_{P}&D_{22}\tau_{P}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
0&0\\
0&1_{\mathcal{N}^{\perp}}\\
\end{bmatrix}.

By Lemma
5.2
,
\hat{\beta}\in\mathcal{N}^{\perp}
and we can write the block matrix
D\tau_{P}
as

D\tau_{P}=\begin{bmatrix}1_{\mathcal{N}}&0\\
0&Q\end{bmatrix},

where
Q
is a contraction and
\mathrm{Ker}(1-Q)=\{0\}
. Therefore, with
{\hat{\beta}}=\overline{\tau}_{P}\beta
,

\begin{bmatrix}a&1\otimes{\hat{\beta}}|_{\mathcal{N}}&1\otimes{\hat{\beta}}|_{\mathcal{N}^{\perp}}\\
0&D_{11}\tau_{P}&D_{12}\tau_{P}\\
\gamma\otimes 1&D_{21}\tau_{P}&D_{22}\tau_{P}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}a&0&1\otimes{\hat{\beta}}\\
0&1_{\mathcal{N}}&0\\
\gamma\otimes 1&0&Q\end{bmatrix}.

Hence

\displaystyle P_{\mathbb{C}\oplus\mathcal{N}^{\perp}}

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}a&1\otimes\beta\\
\gamma\otimes 1&D\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1_{\mathcal{N}}&0\\
0&\tau_{P}\end{bmatrix}P_{\mathbb{C}\oplus\{0\}\oplus\mathcal{N}^{\perp}}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}1&0&0\\
0&0&1\\
\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}a&0&1\otimes{\hat{\beta}}\\
0&1_{\mathcal{N}}&0\\
\gamma\otimes 1&0&Q\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
0&0\\
0&1_{\mathcal{N}^{\perp}}\\
\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}a&1\otimes{\hat{\beta}}\\
\gamma\otimes 1&Q\end{bmatrix}.

Since
L
from equation (
8.28
) and

\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
0&\tau_{P}\end{bmatrix}

are unitary on
\mathbb{C}\oplus\mathcal{L}
respectively, by equation (
8.27
),

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}a&1\otimes{\hat{\beta}}\\
\gamma\otimes 1&Q\end{bmatrix}^{*}

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}a&1\otimes{\hat{\beta}}\\
\gamma\otimes 1&Q\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=P^{*}_{\mathbb{C}\oplus\{0\}\oplus\mathcal{N}^{\perp}}L^{*}\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
0&\overline{\tau}_{P}\end{bmatrix}P^{*}_{\mathbb{C}\oplus\mathcal{N}^{\perp}}P_{\mathbb{C}\oplus\mathcal{N}^{\perp}}L\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
0&\tau_{P}\end{bmatrix}P_{\mathbb{C}\oplus\{0\}\oplus\mathcal{N}^{\perp}}

\displaystyle=1_{\mathbb{C}\oplus\mathcal{N}^{\perp}}

and

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}a&1\otimes{\hat{\beta}}\\
\gamma\otimes 1&Q\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}a&1\otimes{\hat{\beta}}\\
\gamma\otimes 1&Q\end{bmatrix}^{*}

\displaystyle=P_{\mathbb{C}\oplus\mathcal{N}^{\perp}}L\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
0&\tau_{P}\end{bmatrix}P_{\mathbb{C}\oplus\{0\}\oplus\mathcal{N}^{\perp}}P^{*}_{\mathbb{C}\oplus\{0\}\oplus\mathcal{N}^{\perp}}\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
0&\overline{\tau}_{P}\end{bmatrix}L^{*}P^{*}_{\mathbb{C}\oplus\mathcal{N}^{\perp}}

\displaystyle=1_{\mathbb{C}\oplus\mathcal{N}^{\perp}}.

Thus

\begin{bmatrix}a&1\otimes{\hat{\beta}}\\
\gamma\otimes 1&Q\end{bmatrix}

is unitary on
\mathbb{C}\oplus\mathcal{N}^{\perp}
. Hence
(a,{\hat{\beta}},\gamma,Q)
is a generalized realization of the generalized model
(\mathcal{N}^{\perp},u,I)
of
\varphi
. The statement of the theorem holds if we take
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{N}^{\perp}
.∎

Remark 8.5
.

Let us consider the case when
d=2
. Theorem
1.2
states that
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{D}^{2})
if and only if
\varphi\in\mathcal{SA}_{2}
. Let us compare the known results for
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{D}^{2})
from
[
5
]
with our Theorem
8.2
. In
[
5
, Theorem 3.6]
the authors considered a function

\varphi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{D}^{2})
with a carapoint
\tau\in\mathbb{T}^{2}
and used a model
(\mathcal{L},v)
of
\varphi
and a realization
(\alpha,\beta,\gamma,D)
of
(\mathcal{L},v)
to construct an inner generalized model
(\mathcal{M},u,I_{ATY})
, where

I_{ATY}(\lambda)=\frac{\bar{\tau}_{1}\lambda_{1}Y_{1}+\bar{\tau}_{2}\lambda_{2}Y_{2}-\bar{\tau}_{1}\bar{\tau}_{2}\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}1_{\mathcal{M}}}{1_{\mathcal{M}}-\bar{\tau}_{1}\lambda_{1}Y_{2}-\bar{\tau}_{2}\lambda_{2}Y_{1}}\ \text{for}\ \lambda\in\mathbb{D}^{2},

(8.29)

for some pair of positive commuting contractions
Y_{1},Y_{2}
on
\mathcal{M}
such that
Y_{1}+Y_{2}=1_{\mathcal{M}}
. In
Theorem
8.2
, for
\varphi\in\mathcal{SA}_{2}
with a carapoint
\tau\in\mathbb{T}^{2}
, we can use the same model
(\mathcal{L},v)
of
\varphi
and the same realization
(\alpha,\beta,\gamma,D)
of
(\mathcal{L},v)
to prove
the existence of an inner generalized model
(\mathcal{M},u,I)
where
\mathcal{M}
and
u
are the same as before and the
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{M})
-valued inner function
I
is given by

I(\lambda)=1_{\mathcal{M}}-\left(\frac{\boldsymbol{1}}{\boldsymbol{1}-\bar{\tau}\lambda}\right)_{Y}^{-1}\ \text{for}\ \lambda\in\mathbb{D}^{2},

(8.30)

again for some pair
Y=(Y_{1},Y_{2})
of positive contractions summing to
1_{\mathcal{M}}
.
We claim that
I=I_{ATY}
. The operators
Y_{1}
and
Y_{2}=1-Y_{1}
commute, and it is simple to verify that, for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{D}^{2}
,

I_{ATY}(\lambda)=1_{\mathcal{M}}-\frac{(1-\bar{\tau}_{1}\lambda_{1})(1-\bar{\tau}_{2}\lambda_{2})}{1_{\mathcal{M}}-\bar{\tau}_{1}\lambda_{1}Y_{2}-\bar{\tau}_{2}\lambda_{2}Y_{1}}.

(8.31)

Therefore, for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{D}^{2}
,

\displaystyle I(\lambda)=I_{ATY}(\lambda)

\displaystyle\iff\left(\frac{\boldsymbol{1}}{\boldsymbol{1}-\bar{\tau}\lambda}\right)_{Y}^{-1}=\frac{(1-\bar{\tau}_{1}\lambda_{1})(1-\bar{\tau}_{2}\lambda_{2})}{1_{\mathcal{M}}-\bar{\tau}_{1}\lambda_{1}Y_{2}-\bar{\tau}_{2}\lambda_{2}Y_{1}}\

\displaystyle\iff 1_{\mathcal{M}}=\frac{(1-\bar{\tau}_{1}\lambda_{1})(1-\bar{\tau}_{2}\lambda_{2})}{1_{\mathcal{M}}-\bar{\tau}_{1}\lambda_{1}Y_{2}-\bar{\tau}_{2}\lambda_{2}Y_{1}}\left(\frac{\boldsymbol{1}}{\boldsymbol{1}-\bar{\tau}\lambda}\right)_{Y}

\displaystyle\iff 1_{\mathcal{M}}=\frac{(1-\bar{\tau}_{1}\lambda_{1})(1-\bar{\tau}_{2}\lambda_{2})}{1_{\mathcal{M}}-\bar{\tau}_{1}\lambda_{1}Y_{2}-\bar{\tau}_{2}\lambda_{2}Y_{1}}\left(\frac{1}{1-\bar{\tau}_{1}\lambda_{1}}Y_{1}+\frac{1}{1-\bar{\tau}_{2}\lambda_{2}}Y_{2}\right)

\displaystyle\iff 1_{\mathcal{M}}=\frac{1}{1_{\mathcal{M}}-\bar{\tau}_{1}\lambda_{1}Y_{2}-\bar{\tau}_{2}\lambda_{2}Y_{1}}\left((1-\bar{\tau}_{2}\lambda_{2})Y_{1}+(1-\bar{\tau}_{1}\lambda_{1})Y_{2}\right).

Since
Y_{1}+Y_{2}=1_{\mathcal{M}}
, the last equation holds. Hence
I=I_{ATY}
and the two generalized models coincide.

9.
Directional derivatives and slope functions

Let
\varphi\in\mathcal{SA}_{d}
and let
\tau\in\mathbb{T}^{d}
be a carapoint for
\varphi
. Let us describe a set of directions
\delta\in\mathbb{C}^{d}
which point into
\mathbb{D}^{d}
at
\tau
.
For
\tau\in\mathbb{C}^{d}
, define

\mathbb{C}_{+}^{d}(\tau)=\big\{(z_{1},\ldots,z_{d})\in\mathbb{C}^{d}:\mathrm{Re}(z_{j}\overline{\tau}_{j})>0~~\text{for}~~j=1,\ldots,d\big\},

and

\mathbb{C}_{+}(\tau_{j})=\big\{z\in\mathbb{C}:\mathrm{Re}(z\overline{\tau}_{j})>0\big\}~~\text{for}~j=1,\ldots,d.

Observe that
\mathbb{C}^{d}_{+}(\tau)=\mathbb{C}_{+}(\tau_{1})\times\ldots\times\mathbb{C}_{+}(\tau_{d})
. Note that for
t>0
and
\delta\in\mathbb{C}^{d}
,

\lvert\tau_{j}+t\delta_{j}\rvert^{2}=1+2t\mathrm{Re}(\overline{\tau}_{j}\delta_{j})+t^{2}\lvert\delta_{j}\rvert^{2}.

Therefore
\delta
points into
\mathbb{D}^{d}
at
\tau
if and only if
\mathrm{Re}(\overline{\tau}_{j}\delta_{j})<0
for
j=1,\ldots,d
, that is, if and only if
-\delta\in\mathbb{C}^{d}_{+}(\tau)
. Note that
\tau\in\mathbb{C}_{+}^{d}(\tau)
and, if
z\in\mathbb{C}_{+}^{d}(\tau)
, then
\overline{z}\in\mathbb{C}^{d}_{+}(\overline{\tau})
, since

0<\mathrm{Re}(\overline{\tau_{j}}z_{j})=\mathrm{Re}(\tau_{j}\overline{z_{j}}).

Recall Definition
1.14
. Let
\varphi\in\mathcal{SA}_{d}
and let
\tau\in\mathbb{T}^{d}
be a carapoint for the function
\varphi
. Let
\delta\in\mathbb{C}^{d}
point into
\mathbb{D}^{d}
at
\tau
.
\varphi
is differentiable in the direction
\delta\in\mathbb{C}^{d}
at the point
\tau
if there exists a complex number
\omega
of unit modulus such that, whenever
\tau+t\delta\in\mathbb{D}^{d}
for sufficiently small positive
t
,

D_{\delta}\varphi(\tau)=\lim_{t\rightarrow 0^{+}}\dfrac{\varphi(\tau+t\delta)-\omega}{t}~~{\text{exists}}.

Theorem 9.1
.

Let
\tau\in\mathbb{T}^{d}
be a carapoint for
\varphi\in\mathcal{SA}_{d}
.
Then
\varphi
is directionally differentiable at
\tau
and
there exists an analytic function
h:\mathbb{C}_{+}^{d}(\tau)\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
such that

(1)

\mathrm{Re}(-h(z))>0
for all
z\in\mathbb{C}_{+}^{d}(\tau)
;

(2)

h(\tau)=-\liminf_{\lambda{\rightarrow}\tau}\dfrac{1-\lvert\varphi(\lambda)\rvert}{1-\|\lambda\|_{\infty}};

(9.1)

(3)

for all
\delta\in\mathbb{C}_{+}^{d}(\tau)
,

D_{-\delta}\varphi(\tau)=\varphi(\tau)h(\delta),

(9.2)

where
\varphi(\tau)=\lim_{\lambda{\overset{nt}{\rightarrow}}\tau}\varphi(\lambda)
and
|\varphi(\tau)|=1
.

Moreover, we can express the function
h:\mathbb{C}_{+}^{d}(\tau)\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
by

h(z)=-\bigg\langle\bigg(\dfrac{\mathds{1}}{\overline{\tau}z}\bigg)_{Y}^{-1}u(\tau),u(\tau)\bigg\rangle_{\mathcal{M}},~~\text{for all}~z\in\mathbb{C}_{+}^{d}(\tau),

for some desingularized model
(\mathcal{M},u,I)
of
\varphi
with
I
of the form

I(\lambda)=1_{\mathcal{M}}-\bigg(\dfrac{\mathds{1}}{\mathds{1}-\overline{\tau}\lambda}\bigg)_{Y}^{-1}\ \text{for}\ \lambda\in\mathbb{D}^{d},

(9.3)

for some
d
-tuple of positive contractions
(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{d})
on
\mathcal{M}
such that
\sum_{j=1}^{d}Y_{j}=1_{\mathcal{M}}
.

Proof.

By Theorem
8.2
, there exists a desingularized model
(\mathcal{M},u,I)
of
\varphi
relative to
\tau
, with

I(\lambda)=1_{\mathcal{M}}-\bigg(\dfrac{\mathds{1}}{\mathds{1}-\overline{\tau}\lambda}\bigg)^{-1}_{Y},\;\text{ for all }\;\lambda\in\mathbb{D}^{d},

and for some
d
-tuple of positive contractions
(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{d})
on
\mathcal{M}
such that
\sum_{j=1}^{d}Y_{j}=1_{\mathcal{M}}.

By Theorem
8.2
,
\tau
is a
C
-point of the model
(\mathcal{M},u,I)
, so there exists a vector
u(\tau)\in\mathcal{M}
such that

\lim_{\lambda{\overset{nt}{\rightarrow}}\tau}u(\lambda)=u(\tau)

(9.4)

and, for all
\lambda,\mu\in\mathbb{D}^{d}
,

1-\overline{\varphi(\mu)}\varphi(\lambda)=\bigg\langle\Big(1_{\mathcal{M}}-I^{*}(\mu)I(\lambda)\Big)u(\lambda),u(\mu)\bigg\rangle_{\mathcal{M}}.

(9.5)

By Corollary
8.3
,
\varphi
has a non-tangential limit at
\tau
,

\lim_{\lambda{\overset{nt}{\rightarrow}}\tau}\varphi(\lambda)=\omega,

and
|\omega|=1
. We shall define
\varphi(\tau)
to be equal to
\omega
.

Take limits in the last equation as
{\mu{\overset{nt}{\rightarrow}}\tau}
to obtain

1-\overline{\varphi(\tau)}\varphi(\lambda)=\bigg\langle\Big(1_{\mathcal{M}}-I(\lambda)\Big)u(\lambda),u(\tau)\bigg\rangle_{\mathcal{M}}.

(9.6)

On multiplying equation (
9.6
) by
-\varphi(\tau)
, we have

\displaystyle\varphi(\lambda)-\varphi(\tau)

\displaystyle=-\varphi(\tau)\bigg\langle\Big(1_{\mathcal{M}}-I(\lambda)\Big)u(\lambda),u(\tau)\bigg\rangle_{\mathcal{M}}

\displaystyle=\varphi(\tau)\bigg\langle\Big(I(\lambda)-1_{\mathcal{M}}\Big)u(\lambda),u(\tau)\bigg\rangle_{\mathcal{M}}

\displaystyle=\varphi(\tau)\bigg\langle\Big(I(\lambda)-1_{\mathcal{M}}\Big)u(\tau),u(\tau)\bigg\rangle_{\mathcal{M}}

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt+\varphi(\tau)\bigg\langle\Big(I(\lambda)-1_{\mathcal{M}}\Big)\big(u(\lambda)-u(\tau)\big),u(\tau)\bigg\rangle_{\mathcal{M}}.

(9.7)

Let
\delta\in\mathbb{C}_{+}^{d}(\tau)
so that
\lambda_{t}=\tau-t\delta\in\mathbb{D}^{d}
for small enough
t>0
. By Corollary
5.6
,
I(\lambda_{t})
is well defined since
\bigg(\dfrac{\mathds{1}}{\mathds{1}-\overline{\tau}\lambda_{t}}\bigg)_{Y}^{-1}
exists. Note that, for small enough
t
,
\mathrm{Re}(1-t\overline{\tau_{j}}\delta_{j})<1
, that is,
\mathrm{Re}(-t\overline{\tau_{j}}\delta_{j})<0
. Therefore

\displaystyle I(\lambda_{t})-1_{\mathcal{M}}

\displaystyle=1_{\mathcal{M}}-\bigg(\dfrac{\mathds{1}}{\mathds{1}-\overline{\tau}\lambda_{t}}\bigg)_{Y}^{-1}-1_{\mathcal{M}}

\displaystyle=-\bigg(\sum_{j=1}^{d}\dfrac{1}{1-\overline{\tau_{j}}(\tau_{j}-t\delta_{j})}Y_{j}\bigg)^{-1}

\displaystyle=-\bigg(\sum_{j=1}^{d}\dfrac{1}{t\overline{\tau_{j}}\delta_{j}}Y_{j}\bigg)^{-1}=-t\bigg(\dfrac{\mathds{1}}{\overline{\tau}\delta}\bigg)_{Y}^{-1}.

(9.8)

By Theorem
5.5
, equation (
9.8
) produces a well defined operator on
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{M})
, since
\mathrm{Re}(\overline{\tau_{j}}\delta_{j})>0
for
j=1,\ldots,d
. Thus equation (
9.7
) in combination with equation (
9.8
) gives

\displaystyle\dfrac{\varphi(\lambda_{t})-\varphi(\tau)}{t}

\displaystyle=\varphi(\tau)\bigg\langle\dfrac{I(\lambda_{t})-1_{\mathcal{M}}}{t}u(\tau),u(\tau)\bigg\rangle_{\mathcal{M}}

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt+\varphi(\tau)\bigg\langle\dfrac{I(\lambda_{t})-1_{\mathcal{M}}}{t}\big(u(\lambda_{t})-u(\tau)\big),u(\tau)\bigg\rangle_{\mathcal{M}}

\displaystyle=-\varphi(\tau)\bigg\langle\bigg(\dfrac{\mathds{1}}{\overline{\tau}\delta}\bigg)_{Y}^{-1}u(\tau),u(\tau)\bigg\rangle_{\mathcal{M}}

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt-\varphi(\tau)\bigg\langle\bigg(\dfrac{\mathds{1}}{\overline{\tau}\delta}\bigg)_{Y}^{-1}\big(u(\lambda_{t})-u(\tau)\big),u(\tau)\bigg\rangle_{\mathcal{M}}.

By equation (
9.4
),
\lim_{\lambda{\overset{nt}{\rightarrow}}\tau}u(\lambda)=u(\tau)
, thus by letting
t\rightarrow 0^{+}
in the above equation we get that, for all
\delta\in\mathbb{C}_{+}^{d}(\tau)
,

D_{-\delta}\varphi(\tau)=\lim_{t\rightarrow 0^{+}}\dfrac{\varphi(\lambda_{t})-\varphi(\tau)}{t}=-\varphi(\tau)\bigg\langle\bigg(\dfrac{\mathds{1}}{\overline{\tau}\delta}\bigg)_{Y}^{-1}u(\tau),u(\tau)\bigg\rangle_{\mathcal{M}}.

(9.9)

Thus
\varphi
is directionally differentiable at
\tau
.

Let us define the function
h:\mathbb{C}_{+}^{d}(\tau)\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
by

h(z)=-\bigg\langle\bigg(\dfrac{\mathds{1}}{\overline{\tau}z}\bigg)_{Y}^{-1}u(\tau),u(\tau)\bigg\rangle_{\mathcal{M}}~~\text{for all}~z\in\mathbb{C}_{+}^{d}(\tau).

(9.10)

Then

D_{-\delta}\varphi(\tau)=\varphi(\tau)h(\delta),

this proves equation (
9.2
).
Let us prove that
h
is analytic at every point
z\in\mathbb{C}^{d}_{+}(\tau)
. By Proposition
6.2
statement (3) with
\lambda=\overline{\tau}z
, for all
z,x\in\mathbb{C}^{d}_{+}(\tau)
,

\Bigg(\bigg(\dfrac{\mathds{1}}{\overline{\tau}z}\bigg)_{Y}^{-1}\Bigg)^{\prime}(x)=\bigg(\dfrac{\mathds{1}}{\overline{\tau}z}\bigg)_{Y}^{-1}\bigg(\dfrac{x}{\overline{\tau}z^{2}}\bigg)_{Y}\bigg(\dfrac{\mathds{1}}{\overline{\tau}z}\bigg)_{Y}^{-1}.

Recall that
h
is defined by equation (
9.10
), and so, one can show that

h^{\prime}(z)(x)=-\bigg\langle\Bigg(\bigg(\dfrac{\mathds{1}}{\overline{\tau}z}\bigg)_{Y}^{-1}\Bigg)^{\prime}(x)u(\tau),u(\tau)\bigg\rangle_{\mathcal{M}},\;\text{for all}\;x\in\mathbb{C}^{d}_{+}(\tau).

Hence
h
is analytic on
\mathbb{C}_{+}^{d}(\tau)
and

h^{\prime}(z)(x)=-\bigg\langle\bigg(\dfrac{\mathds{1}}{\overline{\tau}z}\bigg)_{Y}^{-1}\bigg(\dfrac{x}{\overline{\tau}z^{2}}\bigg)_{Y}\bigg(\dfrac{\mathds{1}}{\overline{\tau}z}\bigg)_{Y}^{-1}u(\tau),u(\tau)\bigg\rangle_{\mathcal{M}}.

Let us prove that

h(\tau)=-\liminf_{\lambda{\rightarrow}\tau}\dfrac{1-\lvert\varphi(\lambda)\rvert}{1-\|\lambda\|_{\infty}}.

Note that, for
\tau\in\mathbb{C}_{+}^{d}(\tau)
,

\bigg(\dfrac{\mathds{1}}{\overline{\tau}\tau}\bigg)^{-1}_{Y}=\bigg(\sum_{j=1}^{d}Y_{j}\bigg)^{-1}=1_{\mathcal{M}}.

Thus

h(\tau)=-\bigg\langle\bigg(\dfrac{\mathds{1}}{\overline{\tau}\tau}\bigg)^{-1}_{Y}u(\tau),u(\tau)\bigg\rangle_{\mathcal{M}}=-\|u(\tau)\|^{2}_{\mathcal{M}}.

(9.11)

From the model equation (
9.5
), for any
\lambda\in\mathbb{D}^{d}
,

1-\lvert\varphi(\lambda)\rvert^{2}=\|u(\lambda)\|^{2}_{\mathcal{M}}-\|I(\lambda)u(\lambda)\|^{2}_{\mathcal{M}}.

(9.12)

Let
\lambda_{t}=\tau-t\tau
for
t>0
. Note that

\displaystyle I(\lambda_{t})

\displaystyle=1_{\mathcal{M}}-\bigg(\dfrac{\mathds{1}}{\mathds{1}-\overline{\tau}\lambda_{t}}\bigg)_{Y}^{-1}

\displaystyle=1_{\mathcal{M}}-\bigg(\sum_{j=1}^{d}\dfrac{1}{1-\overline{\tau_{j}}(\tau_{j}-t\tau_{j})}Y_{j}\bigg)^{-1}

\displaystyle=1_{\mathcal{M}}-\bigg(\sum_{j=1}^{d}\dfrac{1}{t}Y_{j}\bigg)^{-1}

\displaystyle=1_{\mathcal{M}}-t\bigg(\sum_{j=1}^{d}Y_{j}\bigg)^{-1}=(1-t)\cdot 1_{\mathcal{M}}.

(9.13)

For small enough
t>0
, by equations (
9.12
) and (
9.13
),

\displaystyle 1-\lvert\varphi(\lambda_{t})\rvert^{2}

\displaystyle=\|u(\lambda_{t})\|_{\mathcal{M}}^{2}-\|I(\lambda_{t})u(\lambda_{t})\|_{\mathcal{M}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\|u(\lambda_{t})\|_{\mathcal{M}}^{2}-\|(1-t)u(\lambda_{t})\|^{2}_{\mathcal{M}}

\displaystyle=(2t-t^{2})\|u(\lambda_{t})\|^{2}_{\mathcal{M}}.

This implies

\lvert\varphi(\lambda_{t})\rvert^{2}=1-(2t-t^{2})\|u(\lambda_{t})\|^{2}_{\mathcal{M}}.

(9.14)

For
\lambda_{t}=\tau-t\tau
, we also have

\|\lambda_{t}\|_{\infty}=\|\tau-t\tau\|_{\infty}=(1-t)\|\tau\|_{\infty}=1-t,

and so
1-\|\lambda_{t}\|^{2}_{\infty}=2t-t^{2}>0
for small
t
. Hence

\lim_{t\rightarrow 0^{+}}\dfrac{1-\lvert\varphi(\lambda_{t})\rvert^{2}}{1-\|\lambda_{t}\|^{2}_{\infty}}=\|u(\tau)\|^{2}_{\mathcal{M}}.

Note equation (
9.14
) implies that

\lvert\varphi(\lambda_{t})\rvert=\big(1-(2t-t^{2})\|u(\lambda_{t})\|^{2}_{\mathcal{M}}\big)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Thus we have

\displaystyle\lim_{t\rightarrow 0^{+}}\dfrac{1-\lvert\varphi(\lambda_{t})\rvert}{1-\|\lambda_{t}\|_{\infty}}

\displaystyle=\lim_{t\rightarrow 0^{+}}\dfrac{\big(1-\lvert\varphi(\lambda_{t})\rvert^{2}\big)}{\big(1-\|\lambda_{t}\|^{2}_{\infty}\big)}\dfrac{\big(1+\|\lambda_{t}\|_{\infty}\big)}{\big(1+\lvert\varphi(\lambda_{t})\rvert\big)}

\displaystyle=\lim_{t\rightarrow 0^{+}}\dfrac{\big(1-\lvert\varphi(\lambda_{t})\rvert^{2}\big)}{\big(1-\|\lambda_{t}\|^{2}_{\infty}\big)}\dfrac{\big(2-t\big)}{\big(1+\big(1-(2t-t^{2})\|u(\lambda_{t})\|^{2}_{\mathcal{M}}\big)^{\frac{1}{2}}\big)}

\displaystyle=\lim_{t\rightarrow 0^{+}}\dfrac{1-\lvert\varphi(\lambda_{t})\rvert^{2}}{1-\|\lambda_{t}\|^{2}_{\infty}}\lim_{t\rightarrow 0^{+}}\dfrac{2-t}{1+\big(1-(2t-t^{2})\|u(\lambda_{t})\|^{2}_{\mathcal{M}}\big)^{\frac{1}{2}}}

\displaystyle=\lim_{t\rightarrow 0^{+}}\dfrac{1-\lvert\varphi(\lambda_{t})\rvert^{2}}{1-\|\lambda_{t}\|^{2}_{\infty}}

\displaystyle=\lim_{t\rightarrow 0^{+}}\|u(\lambda_{t})\|^{2}_{\mathcal{M}}=\|u(\tau)\|_{\mathcal{M}}^{2}.

Hence, by equation (
9.11
),

h(\tau)=-\lim_{t\rightarrow 0^{+}}\dfrac{1-\lvert\varphi(\lambda_{t})\rvert}{1-\|\lambda_{t}\|_{\infty}}.

Moreover, since
\tau
is a carapoint of
\varphi
,

\lim_{t\rightarrow 0^{+}}\dfrac{1-\lvert\varphi(\lambda_{t})\rvert}{1-\|\lambda_{t}\|_{\infty}}=\liminf_{\lambda\rightarrow\tau}\dfrac{1-\lvert\varphi(\lambda)\rvert}{1-\|\lambda\|_{\infty}}.

Thus equation (
9.1
) follows.
It remains to show that
\mathrm{Re}(h(z))<0
. For all
z\in\mathbb{C}^{d}_{+}(\tau)
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Re}(-h(z))

\displaystyle=\dfrac{1}{2}\bigg[\bigg\langle\bigg(\dfrac{\mathds{1}}{\overline{\tau}z}\bigg)_{Y}^{-1}u(\tau),u(\tau)\bigg\rangle_{\mathcal{M}}+\overline{\bigg\langle\bigg(\dfrac{\mathds{1}}{\overline{\tau}z}\bigg)_{Y}^{-1}u(\tau),u(\tau)\bigg\rangle_{\mathcal{M}}}\bigg]

\displaystyle=\dfrac{1}{2}\bigg[\bigg\langle\bigg(\dfrac{\mathds{1}}{\overline{\tau}z}\bigg)_{Y}^{-1}u(\tau),u(\tau)\bigg\rangle_{\mathcal{M}}+\bigg\langle u(\tau),\bigg(\dfrac{\mathds{1}}{\overline{\tau}z}\bigg)_{Y}^{-1}u(\tau)\bigg\rangle_{\mathcal{M}}\bigg]

\displaystyle=\dfrac{1}{2}\bigg[\bigg\langle\bigg(\dfrac{\mathds{1}}{\overline{\tau}z}\bigg)_{Y}^{-1}u(\tau),u(\tau)\bigg\rangle_{\mathcal{M}}+\bigg\langle\bigg(\dfrac{\mathds{1}}{\tau\overline{z}}\bigg)_{Y}^{-1}u(\tau),u(\tau)\bigg\rangle_{\mathcal{M}}\bigg]

\displaystyle=\bigg\langle\dfrac{1}{2}\bigg[\bigg(\dfrac{\mathds{1}}{\overline{\tau}z}\bigg)_{Y}^{-1}+\bigg(\dfrac{\mathds{1}}{\tau\overline{z}}\bigg)_{Y}^{-1}\bigg]u(\tau),u(\tau)\bigg\rangle_{\mathcal{M}}

\displaystyle=\bigg\langle\mathrm{Re}\bigg(\bigg(\dfrac{\mathds{1}}{\overline{\tau}z}\bigg)_{Y}^{-1}\bigg)u(\tau),u(\tau)\bigg\rangle_{\mathcal{M}}.

(9.15)

Furthermore, for all
z\in\mathbb{C}^{d}_{+}(\tau)
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Re}\bigg(\bigg(\dfrac{\mathds{1}}{\overline{\tau}z}\bigg)_{Y}^{-1}\bigg)

\displaystyle=\dfrac{1}{2}\bigg[\bigg(\dfrac{\mathds{1}}{\overline{\tau}z}\bigg)_{Y}^{-1}+\bigg(\dfrac{\mathds{1}}{\tau\overline{z}}\bigg)_{Y}^{-1}\bigg]

\displaystyle=\dfrac{1}{2}\bigg[\bigg(\dfrac{\mathds{1}}{\tau\overline{z}}\bigg)_{Y}^{-1}\bigg(\bigg(\dfrac{\mathds{1}}{\tau\overline{z}}\bigg)_{Y}\bigg(\dfrac{\mathds{1}}{\overline{\tau}z}\bigg)_{Y}^{-1}+1_{\mathcal{M}}\bigg)\bigg]

\displaystyle=\dfrac{1}{2}\bigg[\bigg(\dfrac{\mathds{1}}{\tau\overline{z}}\bigg)_{Y}^{-1}\bigg(\bigg(\dfrac{\mathds{1}}{\tau\overline{z}}\bigg)_{Y}+\bigg(\dfrac{\mathds{1}}{\overline{\tau}z}\bigg)_{Y}\bigg)\bigg(\dfrac{\mathds{1}}{\overline{\tau}z}\bigg)_{Y}^{-1}\bigg]

\displaystyle=\bigg(\dfrac{\mathds{1}}{\tau\overline{z}}\bigg)_{Y}^{-1}\mathrm{Re}\bigg(\bigg(\dfrac{\mathds{1}}{\overline{\tau}z}\bigg)_{Y}\bigg)\bigg(\dfrac{\mathds{1}}{\overline{\tau}z}\bigg)_{Y}^{-1}.

It remains to show that
\mathrm{Re}\bigg(\bigg(\dfrac{\mathds{1}}{\overline{\tau}z}\bigg)_{Y}\bigg)
is positive definite. By Lemma
5.3
,
Y_{j}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{M})
satisfies
0\leq Y_{j}\leq 1
for
j=1,\ldots,d
. Thus, for all
z\in\mathbb{C}^{d}_{+}(\tau)
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Re}\bigg(\bigg(\dfrac{\mathds{1}}{\overline{\tau}z}\bigg)_{Y}\bigg)

\displaystyle=\dfrac{1}{2}\bigg[\bigg(\dfrac{\mathds{1}}{\overline{\tau}z}\bigg)_{Y}+\bigg(\dfrac{\mathds{1}}{\tau\overline{z}}\bigg)_{Y}\Big]

\displaystyle=\dfrac{1}{2}\sum_{j=1}^{d}\dfrac{1}{\overline{\tau_{j}}z_{j}}Y_{j}+\sum_{j=1}^{d}\dfrac{1}{\tau_{j}\overline{z_{j}}}Y_{j}

\displaystyle=\dfrac{1}{2}\sum_{j=1}^{d}\dfrac{\overline{\tau_{j}}z_{j}+\tau_{j}\overline{z_{j}}}{\lvert z_{j}\rvert^{2}}Y_{j}=\sum_{j=1}^{d}\dfrac{\mathrm{Re}(\overline{\tau_{j}}z_{j})}{\lvert z_{j}\rvert^{2}}Y_{j}.

Since
z\in\mathbb{C}^{d}_{+}(\tau)
,
\mathrm{Re}(\overline{\tau_{j}}z_{j})>0
. Hence
\mathrm{Re}\bigg(\bigg(\dfrac{\mathds{1}}{\overline{\tau}z}\bigg)_{Y}\bigg)
is the sum of positive definite operators and in turn
\mathrm{Re}\bigg(\bigg(\dfrac{\mathds{1}}{\overline{\tau}z}\bigg)^{-1}_{Y}\bigg)>0
. Thus
\mathrm{Re}(-h(z))
is strictly positive for all
z\in\mathbb{C}_{+}^{d}(\tau)
.∎

We shall call the function
h
described in Theorem
9.1

the
slope function
of
\varphi
at the point
\tau
. Thus
h
is the slope function of
\varphi
at a carapoint
\tau\in\mathbb{T}^{d}

if, for all
\delta\in\mathbb{C}_{+}^{d}(\tau)
,

D_{-\delta}\varphi(\tau)=\varphi(\tau)h(\delta).

(9.16)

Remark 9.2
.

Let
\varphi\in\mathcal{SA}_{d}
and let
\tau\in\mathbb{T}^{d}
. It is clear that the existence of a slope function
h
of
\varphi
at
\tau
implies that

\liminf_{\lambda\to\tau,\lambda\in\mathbb{D}^{d}}\frac{1-|\varphi(\lambda)|}{1-\|\lambda\|_{\infty}}=-h(\tau)<\infty,

(9.17)

and so
\tau
is a carapoint for
\varphi
.

Question 9.3
.

Let
d>2
,
\tau\in\mathbb{T}^{d}
and let
h:\mathbb{C}_{+}^{d}(\tau)\to\mathbb{C}
be analytic function. Under which conditions on
h
, is
h
a slope function of some function
\varphi\in\mathcal{SA}_{d}
at a carapoint
\tau
?

In the case
d=2
, the answer is given in

[
5
, Theorem 6.2]
.

10.
An example of a function
\varphi_{3}\in\mathcal{SA}_{3}
with a carapoint

Note that Theorem
9.1
can be used to find directional derivatives for Schur-Agler class functions that have a carapoint
\tau\in\mathbb{T}^{d}
but need not necessarily be continuous at
\tau
. In this section, we shall apply this theorem to the function

\varphi_{3}(\lambda)=\dfrac{3\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}\lambda_{3}-\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{1}\lambda_{3}-\lambda_{2}\lambda_{3}}{3-\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{3}}\ \quad\text{for}\ \lambda\in\mathbb{D}^{3}.

We will need the following set.

Definition 10.1
.

The symmetrized tridisc
\mathbb{G}_{3}
is the set

\mathbb{G}_{3}=\Big\{(\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{3},\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{2}\lambda_{3}+\lambda_{1}\lambda_{3},\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}\lambda_{3}):\lambda_{1}\in\mathbb{D},\lambda_{2}\in\mathbb{D},\lambda_{3}\in\mathbb{D}\Big\}.

Alternatively, let
e_{j}
denote the elementary symmetric function of degree
j
in three variables for
j=1,2,3
. Thus

\displaystyle e_{1}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})

\displaystyle=\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{3},

\displaystyle e_{2}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})

\displaystyle=\lambda_{2}\lambda_{3}+\lambda_{3}\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2},

\displaystyle e_{3}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})

\displaystyle=\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}\lambda_{3},

(10.1)

and

\mathbb{G}_{3}=\bigg\{(e_{1}(\lambda),e_{2}(\lambda),e_{3}(\lambda)):\lambda\in\mathbb{D}^{3}\bigg\}.

Example 10.2
.

Let
\varphi_{3}:\mathbb{D}^{3}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
be the function

\varphi_{3}(\lambda)=\dfrac{3\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}\lambda_{3}-\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{2}\lambda_{3}-\lambda_{1}\lambda_{3}}{3-\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{3}}.

(10.2)

The following conditions are satisfied:

(1)

\varphi_{3}\in\mathcal{SA}_{3}
and
\varphi_{3}
is inner;

(2)

\mathds{1}=(1,1,1)\in\mathbb{T}^{3}
is a carapoint of
\varphi_{3}
;

(3)

\varphi_{3}
does not extend continuously to a neighbourhood of the point
\mathds{1}
;

(4)

there exists a desingularized model
(\mathcal{M},u,I)
for
\varphi_{3}
at
\mathds{1}
. Furthermore,
\varphi_{3}
has a directional derivative
D_{-\delta}\varphi_{3}(\mathds{1})
for all directions
\delta\in\mathbb{C}^{3}_{+}(\mathds{1})
and

D_{-\delta}\varphi_{3}(\mathds{1})=\varphi_{3}(\mathds{1})h(\delta)

where

h(\delta)=-\bigg\langle\bigg(\dfrac{\mathds{1}}{\mathds{1}\delta}\bigg)_{Y}^{-1}u(\mathds{1}),u(\mathds{1})\bigg\rangle_{\mathcal{M}}~~\text{for all}~\delta\in\mathbb{C}_{+}^{3}(\mathds{1}).

Proof.

(1)
Recall that in
[
17
]
, Knese proved that
\varphi_{3}\in\mathcal{SA}_{3}
, and by Rudin
[
18
, Theorem 5.2.5]
,
\varphi_{3}
is inner.

(2) If
0<r<1
and
\lambda=r\mathds{1}
then

\varphi_{3}(\lambda)=\varphi_{3}(r\mathds{1})=\dfrac{3r^{3}-3r^{2}}{3-3r}=-r^{2},

and so

\frac{1-|\varphi_{3}(\lambda)|}{1-\|\lambda\|_{\infty}}=\frac{1-r^{2}}{1-r}=1+r\leq 2,

thus

\liminf_{\lambda\to\mathds{1}}\frac{1-|\varphi_{3}(\lambda)|}{1-\|\lambda\|_{\infty}}<\infty,

which is to say that
\mathds{1}
is a carapoint for
\varphi_{3}
.
Observe also that
\varphi_{3}
has radial limit
-1
at
\mathds{1}
,

\lim_{r\rightarrow 1^{-}}\varphi_{3}(r\mathds{1})=-1.

(10.3)

(3) Now suppose
\varphi_{3}
does have a continuous extension
\psi
to
\mathbb{D}^{3}\cup U
for some open neighborhood
U
of
\mathds{1}
. Then

\psi(\mathds{1})=\lim_{r\rightarrow 1^{-}}\psi(r\mathds{1})=\lim_{r\rightarrow 1^{-}}\varphi_{3}(r\mathds{1})=-1,

and since
\psi
is continuous on
\mathbb{D}^{3}\cup U
it follows that, for any path
(\lambda(t))_{(0<t<1)}
in
\mathbb{D}^{3}
such that
\lambda(t)\rightarrow\mathds{1}
as
t\rightarrow 0
,
\varphi_{3}(\lambda(t))=\psi(\lambda(t))\rightarrow\psi(\mathds{1})=-1
. Hence, to show that
\varphi_{3}
does not extend continuously to any neighbourhood of
\mathds{1}
, it suffices to construct a path
(\lambda(t))_{(0<t<1)}
in
\mathbb{D}^{3}
, which need not be continuous, such that
\lambda(t)\rightarrow\mathds{1}
as
t\rightarrow 0
and
\varphi_{3}(\lambda(t))\nrightarrow-1
as
t\rightarrow 0
.

We can write
\varphi_{3}
in the following form

\varphi_{3}(\lambda)=\dfrac{3e_{3}(\lambda)-e_{2}(\lambda)}{3-e_{1}(\lambda)}~~\text{for}~\lambda\in\mathbb{D}^{3},

where
e_{j}
for
j=1,2,3
are defined by equations (
10.1
).

If we define
f:\mathbb{G}_{3}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
by
f(s_{1},s_{2},s_{3})=\dfrac{3s_{3}-s_{2}}{3-s_{1}}
then
\varphi_{3}(\lambda)=f(e_{1}(\lambda),e_{2}(\lambda),e_{3}(\lambda))
for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{D}^{3}
. Note also that
\lambda
can be recovered (up to a permutation of its entries) from the triple
(e_{1}(\lambda),e_{2}(\lambda),e_{3}(\lambda))
, since
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3}
are the zeros of the polynomial

z^{3}-e_{1}(\lambda)z^{2}+e_{2}(\lambda)z-e_{3}(\lambda)

in
z
. We shall construct a path
s(t)
such that
0<t<1
in
\mathbb{G}_{3}
by using a parametrization of the points of
\mathbb{G}_{3}
due to Costara,
[
10
, Section 2.5]
. Note that, for any
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}^{3}
,
(e_{1}(\lambda),e_{2}(\lambda),e_{3}(\lambda))\in\mathbb{G}_{3}
if and only if the roots of the equation

z^{3}-e_{1}(\lambda)z^{2}+e_{2}(\lambda)z-e_{3}(\lambda)=0

all lie in
\mathbb{D}
, that is, if and only if
\lambda\in\mathbb{D}^{3}
.

From
[
16
]
, a point
(s_{1},s_{2},s_{3})\in\mathbb{C}^{3}
belongs to
\mathbb{G}_{3}
if and only if
s_{3}\in\mathbb{D}
and there exists
(z_{1},z_{2})\in\mathbb{G}
such that

s_{1}=z_{1}+\overline{z}_{2}s_{3}~~\text{and}~~s_{2}=z_{2}+\overline{z_{1}}s_{3}.

(10.4)

It is also known that
(z_{1},z_{2})\in\mathbb{G}
if and only if
z_{2}\in\mathbb{D}
and there exists
\beta\in\mathbb{D}
such that
z_{1}=\beta+\overline{\beta}z_{2}
.

For
t\in(0,1)
, choose a point
z(t)\in\mathbb{G}
by taking
\beta=z_{2}=1-t
, that is,

z_{1}(t)=1-t+(\overline{1-t})(1-t)~~\text{and}~~z_{2}(t)=1-t.

Thus

z(t)=\bigg((1-t)(2-t),1-t\bigg).

Thence construct a point
s(t)\in\mathbb{G}_{3}
by taking
(z_{1},z_{2})=z(t)
,
s_{3}=1-t
in the parametrization (
10.4
). We obtain

\displaystyle s(t)

\displaystyle=\bigg((1-t)(2-t)+(1-t)^{2},1-t+\overline{(1-t)(2-t)}(1-t),(1-t)\bigg)

\displaystyle=\bigg((1-t)(3-2t),(1-t)(1+(1-t)(2-t)),(1-t)\bigg)

(10.5)

Now lift the path
s(t)
such that
0<t<1
in
\mathbb{G}_{3}
to a path
\lambda(t)
for
0<t<1
in
\mathbb{D}^{3}
as follows.

Since
s(t)\in\mathbb{G}_{3}
, the three roots of the cubic equation

z^{3}-e_{1}(\lambda)z^{2}+e_{2}(\lambda)z-e_{3}(\lambda)=0

(10.6)

all lie in
\mathbb{D}
. For each
t\in(0,1)
, let
\lambda_{1}(t)
be the root of the equation (
10.6
) which has minimum distance from
-1
and let
\lambda_{2}(t),\lambda_{3}(t)
be the other two roots of equation (
10.6
), numbered so that

|1-\lambda_{1}(t)|\leq|1-\lambda_{2}(t)|\leq|1-\lambda_{3}(t)|.

(10.7)

Then, for each
t
and all
z\in\mathbb{C}
,

(z-\lambda_{1}(t))(z-\lambda_{2}(t))(z-\lambda_{3}(t))=z^{3}-s_{1}(t)z^{2}+s_{2}(t)z-s_{3}(t).

(10.8)

Putting
z=1
, we have

(1-\lambda_{1}(t))(1-\lambda_{2}(t))(1-\lambda_{3}(t))=1-s_{1}(t)+s_{2}(t)-s_{3}(t).

Take absolute values and use the fact that we chose
\lambda_{1}(t)
so that
|1-\lambda_{1}(t)|\leq|1-\lambda_{j}(t)|
for
j=2,3
to deduce that

|1-\lambda_{1}(t)|^{3}\leq|1-\lambda_{1}(t)||1-\lambda_{2}(t)||1-\lambda_{3}(t)|=|1-s_{1}(t)+s_{2}(t)-s_{3}(t)|.

From equation (
10.5
) we can see that
s(t)\rightarrow(3,3,1)
as
t\rightarrow 0
and hence
1-s_{1}(t)+s_{2}(t)-s_{3}(t)\rightarrow 0
. It follows that
\lambda_{1}(t)\rightarrow 1
as
t\rightarrow 0
.

In the light of equation (
10.8
), the function

q_{t}(z)=\dfrac{z^{3}-s_{1}(t)z^{2}+s_{2}(t)z-s_{3}(t)}{z-\lambda_{1}(t)}

is a monic quadratic polynomial in
z
for each
t\in(0,1)
: let us write

q_{t}(z)=z^{2}-b_{1}(t)z+b_{0}(t).

Then

(z-\lambda_{1}(t))(z^{2}-b_{1}(t)z+b_{0}(t))=z^{3}-s_{1}(t)z^{2}+s_{2}(t)z-s_{3}(t).

Thus

\lambda_{1}(t)+b_{1}(t)=s_{1}(t),~~\lambda_{1}(t)b_{1}(t)+b_{0}(t)=s_{2}(t),~~\lambda_{1}(t)b_{0}(t)=s_{2}(t).

As
t\rightarrow 0
,
\lambda_{1}(t)\rightarrow 1
,
s(t)\rightarrow(3,3,1)
, and so
b_{1}(t)\rightarrow 2
,
b_{0}(t)\rightarrow 1
. Therefore

q_{t}(z)\rightarrow z^{2}-2z+1~~\text{as}~t\rightarrow 0~~\text{for all}~z\in\mathbb{C}.

Note that
\lambda_{2}(t),\lambda_{3}(t)
are the zeros of the monic quadratic polynomial
q_{t}(z)
. We have numbered them so that
|1-\lambda_{2}(t)|\leq|1-\lambda_{3}(t)|
, see equation (
10.7
). Then, for all
t\in(0,1)
,

|1-\lambda_{2}(t)|^{2}\leq|1-\lambda_{2}(t)||1-\lambda_{3}(t)|=|q_{t}(1)|,

and since
q_{t}(1)\rightarrow 0
as
t\rightarrow 0
, it follows that

\lambda_{2}(t)\rightarrow 1~~\text{as}~t\rightarrow 0.

Furthermore,

\lambda_{1}(t)\lambda_{2}(t)\lambda_{3}(t)=s_{3}(t)

for all t and
s_{3}(t)\rightarrow 1
as
t\rightarrow 0
, we have
\lambda_{3}(t)\rightarrow 1
as
t\rightarrow 0
. Hence

\lambda(t)=(\lambda_{1}(t),\lambda_{2}(t),\lambda_{3}(t))\rightarrow(1,1,1)~~\text{as}~t\rightarrow 0.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\varphi_{3}(\lambda(t))

\displaystyle=\dfrac{3s_{3}(t)-s_{2}(t)}{3-s_{1}(t)}

\displaystyle=\dfrac{3(1-t)-(1-t)(3-3t+t^{2})}{3-(3-5t+2t^{2})}

\displaystyle=\dfrac{(1-t)(3t-t^{2})}{5t-2t^{2}}=\dfrac{(1-t)(3-t)}{5-2t}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\dfrac{3}{5}~~\text{as}~t\rightarrow 0.

Hence
\varphi_{3}
has no continuous extension to any neighbourhood of
\mathds{1}
.

(4) By Theorem
8.2
, there exists a desingularized model
(\mathcal{M},u,I)
for
\varphi_{3}
at
\mathds{1}
. By Theorem
9.1
, there exists an analytic function
h:\mathbb{C}^{3}_{+}(\mathds{1})\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
given by

h(z)=-\bigg\langle\bigg(\dfrac{\mathds{1}}{\mathds{1}z}\bigg)_{Y}u(\mathds{1}),u(\mathds{1})\bigg\rangle_{\mathcal{M}}

for all
z\in\mathbb{C}^{3}_{+}(\mathds{1})
, for some
3
-tuple of positive contractions
Y=(Y_{1},Y_{2},Y_{3})
on
\mathcal{M}
for which
Y_{1}+Y_{2}+Y_{3}=1_{\mathcal{M}}
such that, for all
\delta\in\mathbb{C}^{3}(\mathds{1})
,
\varphi_{3}
is differentiable in the direction
-\delta
at the point
\mathds{1}
with

D_{-\delta}\varphi_{3}(\mathds{1})=\varphi_{3}(\mathds{1})h(\delta).

∎

Remark 10.3
.

Knese,
[
17
]
, also proved that every Schur-Agler class rational inner function has a finite-dimensional unitary realization and that the function
\varphi_{3}
given by equation (
10.2
) has a
9
-dimensional unitary realization. Knese,
[
17
]
, proved that this function
\varphi_{3}
cannot be realized with dimension less than
6
.

11.
A model and a unitary realization for
\varphi_{3}

In the dissertation of C. Evans (see
[
12
, Sections 7.3, 7.4]
) an explicit
9
-dimensional model
(\mathcal{L},v)
of
\varphi_{3}
given by equation (
10.2
) is computed and the corresponding realization is presented. In this model
(\mathcal{L},v)
,
\mathds{1}
is a
B
-point but not a
C
-point.

Knese,
[
17
]
, proved that every Schur-Agler class rational inner function has a finite-dimensional unitary realization, Definition
2.12
. We state this in the result below.

Theorem 11.1
.

[
17
, Theorem 2.9]

Let
f:\mathbb{D}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{D}
be a Schur-Agler class rational inner function and write
f=p/q
, with
p,q\in\mathbb{C}[z_{1},\ldots,z_{d}]
. Then

(1)

a sum-of-squares decomposition holds. There exist integers
N_{1},\ldots,N_{d}
such that

\lvert q(z)\rvert^{2}-\lvert p(z)\rvert^{2}=\sum_{j=1}^{d}(1-\lvert z_{j}\rvert^{2})\sum_{k=1}^{N_{j}}\lvert A_{j,k}(z)\rvert^{2}

where
A_{j,k}\in\mathbb{C}[z_{1},\ldots,z_{d}]
.

(2)

f
has a finite-dimensional unitary realization.

We are going to use Knese’s results from
[
17
, Section 4]
, to get a model and a unitary realization of
\varphi_{3}
. Note that the function
\varphi_{3}
, defined above by the equation
(
10.2
),
can be presented as
\varphi_{3}(\lambda)=\dfrac{p(\lambda)}{q(\lambda)}
, for
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})\in\mathbb{D}^{3}
, where

\displaystyle p(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})

\displaystyle=3\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}\lambda_{3}-\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{1}\lambda_{3}-\lambda_{2}\lambda_{3},

\displaystyle q(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})

\displaystyle=3-\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{3}.

Knese showed in
[
17
]
that, for
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})\in\mathbb{D}^{3}
,

\displaystyle\lvert

\displaystyle 3-\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{3}\rvert^{2}-\lvert 3\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}\lambda_{3}-\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{1}\lambda_{3}-\lambda_{2}\lambda_{3}\rvert^{2}

\displaystyle=(1-\lvert\lambda_{1}\rvert^{2})S(\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})+(1-\lvert\lambda_{2}\rvert^{2})S(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{3})+(1-\lvert\lambda_{3}\rvert^{2})S(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}).

(11.1)

Here, for
\eta,\zeta\in\mathbb{C}
,

S(\eta,\zeta)=\lvert s_{1}(\eta,\zeta)\rvert^{2}+\lvert s_{2}(\eta,\zeta)\rvert^{2}+\lvert s_{3}(\eta,\zeta)\rvert^{2},

where

\displaystyle s_{1}(\eta,\zeta)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{3}\Big(\eta\zeta-\dfrac{1}{2}\eta-\dfrac{1}{2}\zeta\Big)

\displaystyle s_{2}(\eta,\zeta)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{3}\Big(1-\dfrac{1}{2}\eta-\dfrac{1}{2}\zeta\Big)

\displaystyle s_{3}(\eta,\zeta)

\displaystyle=\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\eta-\zeta).

Define the map
v:\mathbb{D}^{3}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{9}
by

v(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})=\dfrac{1}{q(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})}\begin{bmatrix}w(\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})\\
w(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{3})\\
w(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})\end{bmatrix},

(11.2)

where, for
\eta,\zeta\in\mathbb{D}
,

w(\eta,\zeta)=\begin{bmatrix}s_{1}(\eta,\zeta)\\
s_{2}(\eta,\zeta)\\
s_{3}(\eta,\zeta)\end{bmatrix}.

Note that, for
\eta,\zeta\in\mathbb{D}
,

S(\eta,\zeta)=\|w(\eta,\zeta)\|_{\mathbb{C}^{3}}^{2}.

With respect to the decomposition
\mathbb{C}^{9}=\mathbb{C}^{3}\oplus\mathbb{C}^{3}\oplus\mathbb{C}^{3}
, define
P_{1},P_{2}
and
P_{3}
to be the orthogonal projections onto
\mathbb{C}^{3}\oplus\{0\}\oplus\{0\}
,
\{0\}\oplus\mathbb{C}^{3}\oplus\{0\}
and
\{0\}\oplus\{0\}\oplus\mathbb{C}^{3}
, respectively. For
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})\in\mathbb{D}^{3}
, the map
\lambda_{P}:\mathbb{C}^{9}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{9}
is defined by

\lambda_{P}=\lambda_{1}P_{1}+\lambda_{2}P_{2}+\lambda_{3}P_{3}.

(11.3)

Proposition 11.2
.

(
Polarization theorem for holomorphic functions
)
[
13
, Proposition 1]

Suppose that
\Omega
is a domain in
\mathbb{C}^{d}
and let
\Omega^{*}=\{z\in\mathbb{C}^{d}:\overline{z}\in\Omega\}
be its conjugate domain. Suppose that
H:\Omega\times\Omega^{*}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
is a holomorphic function of
2d
complex variables
(\lambda,\mu)
, and that

H(\lambda,\overline{\lambda})=0

for all
\lambda\in\Omega
. Then

H(\lambda,\mu)=0

for all
(\lambda,\mu)\in\Omega\times\Omega^{*}
.

We show that there exists a model
(\mathbb{C}^{9},v)
for
\varphi_{3}
. For
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})\in\mathbb{D}^{3}
, rewrite equation (
11.1
) as

\displaystyle\lvert q(\lambda)\rvert^{2}

\displaystyle-\lvert p(\lambda)\rvert^{2}=(1-\lvert\lambda_{1}\rvert^{2})\|w(\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})\|_{\mathbb{C}^{3}}^{2}

\displaystyle+(1-\lvert\lambda_{2}\rvert^{2})\|w(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{3})\|_{\mathbb{C}^{3}}^{2}+(1-\lvert\lambda_{3}\rvert^{2})\|w(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})\|_{\mathbb{C}^{3}}^{2},

which is equivalent to

\displaystyle 1-\bigg\lvert

\displaystyle\dfrac{p(\lambda)}{q(\lambda)}\bigg\rvert^{2}=(1-\lvert\lambda_{1}\rvert^{2})\bigg\|\dfrac{w(\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})}{q(\lambda)}\bigg\|_{\mathbb{C}^{3}}^{2}+(1-\lvert\lambda_{2}\rvert^{2})\bigg\|\dfrac{w(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{3})}{q(\lambda)}\bigg\|_{\mathbb{C}^{3}}^{2}

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt+(1-\lvert\lambda_{3}\rvert^{2})\bigg\|\dfrac{w(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})}{q(\lambda)}\bigg\|_{\mathbb{C}^{3}}^{2}~~\text{for all}~\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})\in\mathbb{D}^{3}.

(11.4)

By the polarization theorem for holomorphic functions, Proposition
11.2
, equation (
11.4
), we obtain, for
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})
and
\mu=(\mu_{1},\mu_{2},\mu_{3})
in
\mathbb{D}^{3}
,

\displaystyle 1-\dfrac{\overline{p(\mu)}}{\overline{q(\mu)}}\dfrac{p(\lambda)}{q(\lambda)}=(1-\overline{\mu_{1}}\lambda_{1})\bigg\langle\dfrac{w(\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})}{q(\lambda)},\dfrac{w(\mu_{2},\mu_{3})}{q(\mu)}\bigg\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{3}}

\displaystyle+(1-\overline{\mu_{2}}\lambda_{2})\bigg\langle\dfrac{w(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{3})}{q(\lambda)},\dfrac{w(\mu_{1},\mu_{3})}{q(\mu)}\bigg\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{3}}+(1-\overline{\mu_{3}}\lambda_{3})\bigg\langle\dfrac{w(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})}{q(\lambda)},\dfrac{w(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}{q(\mu)}\bigg\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{3}}.

(11.5)

To see that the relation (
11.5
) holds, note that both sides of equation (
11.5
) are holomorphic functions of
\lambda
and
\overline{\mu}
on
\mathbb{D}^{3}\times\mathbb{D}^{3}
, since
p
and
q
are polynomials,
q\neq 0
in
\mathbb{D}^{3}
and
v
is a rational vector function without poles in
\mathbb{D}^{3}
. If
H(\lambda,\overline{\mu})
denotes the difference between the two sides of equation (
11.5
), then, by equation (
11.4
),
H(\lambda,\overline{\lambda})=0
for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{D}^{3}
. Hence, by Proposition
11.2
,
H(\lambda,\overline{\mu})=0
for all
\lambda,\mu\in\mathbb{D}^{3}
.

We can rewrite the right hand side of equation (
11.5
) as

\Bigg\langle\left(1_{\mathbb{C}^{9}}-\begin{bmatrix}\overline{\mu_{1}}\lambda_{1}P_{1}&&&\\
&\overline{\mu_{2}}\lambda_{2}P_{2}&&\\
&&\overline{\mu_{3}}\lambda_{3}P_{3}\end{bmatrix}\right)\bigg(\dfrac{1}{q(\lambda)}\bigg)\begin{bmatrix}w(\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})\\
w(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{3})\\
w(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})\end{bmatrix},\bigg(\dfrac{1}{q(\mu)}\bigg)\begin{bmatrix}w(\mu_{2},\mu_{3})\\
w(\mu_{1},\mu_{3})\\
w(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})\end{bmatrix}\Bigg\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{9}}.

Hence, for
\lambda,\mu\in\mathbb{D}^{3}
, the right hand side of equation (
11.5
) can be written in the form

\langle(1_{\mathbb{C}^{9}}-\mu^{*}_{P}\lambda_{P})v(\lambda),v(\mu)\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{9}},

where
v
is given by equation (
11.2
).
Therefore
(\mathbb{C}^{9},v)
is a model for
\varphi_{3}
, that is,

1-\overline{\varphi_{3}(\mu)}\varphi_{3}(\lambda)=\langle(1_{\mathbb{C}^{9}}-\mu^{*}_{P}\lambda_{P})v(\lambda),v(\mu)\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{9}}~~\text{for all}~\lambda,\mu\in\mathbb{D}^{3}.

Note that, by Proposition
4.2
and Example
10.2
(2),
\mathds{1}=(1,1,1)
is a
B
-point for
(\mathbb{C}^{9},v)
.

We also construct a finite-dimensional unitary realization
(a,\beta,\gamma,D)
for
\varphi_{3}
, that is, we find a scalar
a
, vectors
\beta,\gamma\in{\mathbb{C}^{9}}
and an operator
D
on
{\mathbb{C}^{9}}
such that

L=\begin{bmatrix}a&1\otimes\beta\\
\gamma\otimes 1&D\end{bmatrix}:\mathbb{C}\oplus\mathbb{C}^{9}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}\oplus\mathbb{C}^{9}

(11.6)

is unitary, and, for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{D}^{3}
,

\varphi_{3}(\lambda)=a+\langle\lambda_{P}(1_{\mathbb{C}^{9}}-D\lambda_{P})^{-1}\gamma,\beta\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{9}}.

(11.7)

Choose
a=\varphi_{3}(0)=0
and find a unitary realization with this choice.
By the lurking isometry argument applied to the model
(\mathbb{C}^{9},v)
of
\varphi_{3}
,
we get

\displaystyle\varphi_{3}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\langle\lambda_{P}v(\lambda),\beta\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{9}}\ \text{and}\

(11.8)

\displaystyle v(\lambda)

\displaystyle=D\lambda_{P}v(\lambda)+\gamma,

(11.9)

for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{D}^{3}
.
To find
\beta
, suppose

\beta=\begin{bmatrix}\beta_{1}\\
\vdots\\
\beta_{9}\end{bmatrix}.

Since
\varphi_{3}(\lambda)=\dfrac{p(\lambda)}{q(\lambda)}
, for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{D}^{3}
, multiply both sides of equation (
11.8
) by
q(\lambda)
to obtain

p(\lambda)=\Bigg\langle\lambda_{1}w(\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3}),\begin{bmatrix}\beta_{1}\\
\beta_{2}\\
\beta_{3}\end{bmatrix}\Bigg\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{3}}+\Bigg\langle\lambda_{2}w(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{3}),\begin{bmatrix}\beta_{4}\\
\beta_{5}\\
\beta_{6}\end{bmatrix}\Bigg\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{3}}+\Bigg\langle\lambda_{3}w(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}),\begin{bmatrix}\beta_{7}\\
\beta_{8}\\
\beta_{9}\end{bmatrix}\Bigg\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{3}}.

Expand out each inner product in the equation above and compare each of the terms
\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}\lambda_{3}
,
\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}
,
\lambda_{1}\lambda_{3}
,
\lambda_{2}\lambda_{3}
,
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3}
and any scalars on the left and right hand side of the equation above to find

\begin{bmatrix}1&0&0&1&0&0&1&0&0\\
-\sqrt{3}&0&\sqrt{2}&-\sqrt{3}&0&\sqrt{2}&0&0&0\\
-\sqrt{3}&0&-\sqrt{2}&0&0&0&-\sqrt{3}&0&\sqrt{2}\\
0&0&0&\sqrt{3}&0&\sqrt{2}&\sqrt{3}&0&\sqrt{2}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\beta_{1}\\
\vdots\\
\beta_{9}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\sqrt{3}\\
-2\\
-2\\
2\end{bmatrix}.

It can be verified, for example by a computer algebra program such as Maple, that the unique solution of this system of equations is

\beta=\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{3}}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\\
0\\
1\\
0\\
0\\
1\\
0\\
0\end{bmatrix}=\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{3}}\begin{bmatrix}e_{1}\\
e_{1}\\
e_{1}\end{bmatrix},

(11.10)

where
e_{1}=(1,0,0)\in\mathbb{C}^{3}
.

We now calculate
\gamma
. By Theorem
11.1
, the matrix
L
from equation (
11.6
) with
a=0
is unitary on
\mathbb{C}\oplus\mathbb{C}^{9}
and satisfies

L\begin{bmatrix}1\\
\lambda_{P}v(\lambda)\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}0&1\otimes\beta\\
\gamma\otimes 1&D\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
\lambda_{P}v(\lambda)\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\varphi_{3}(\lambda)\\
v(\lambda)\end{bmatrix},\ \text{ for all}\ \lambda\in\mathbb{D}^{3}.

(11.11)

On multiplying the above equation on the left by
L^{*}
, we obtain the equation

\begin{bmatrix}1\\
\lambda_{P}v(\lambda)\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}0&1\otimes\gamma\\
\beta\otimes 1&D^{*}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\varphi_{3}(\lambda)\\
v(\lambda)\end{bmatrix},\ \text{ for all}\ \lambda\in\mathbb{D}^{3}.

This is equivalent to the system of equations

\displaystyle 1

\displaystyle=\langle v(\lambda),\gamma\rangle,

(11.12)

\displaystyle\lambda_{P}v(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\varphi_{3}(\lambda)\beta+D^{*}v(\lambda),\ \text{ for all}\ \lambda\in\mathbb{D}^{3}.

Suppose

\gamma=\begin{bmatrix}\gamma_{1}\\
\vdots\\
\gamma_{9}\end{bmatrix}.

Multiply both sides of equation (
11.12
) by
q(\lambda)
to obtain

\displaystyle q(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\Bigg\langle\begin{bmatrix}w(\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})\\
w(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{3})\\
w(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})\end{bmatrix},\begin{bmatrix}\gamma_{1}\\
\vdots\\
\gamma_{9}\end{bmatrix}\Bigg\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{9}}

\displaystyle=\Bigg\langle w(\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3}),\begin{bmatrix}\gamma_{1}\\
\gamma_{2}\\
\gamma_{3}\end{bmatrix}\Bigg\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{9}}+\Bigg\langle w(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{3}),\begin{bmatrix}\gamma_{4}\\
\gamma_{5}\\
\gamma_{6}\end{bmatrix}\Bigg\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{9}}+\Bigg\langle w(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}),\begin{bmatrix}\gamma_{7}\\
\gamma_{8}\\
\gamma_{9}\end{bmatrix}\Bigg\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{9}},

for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{D}^{3}
.
As in the method for finding
\beta
, we expand each inner product in the equation above and compare each of the terms
\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}\lambda_{3}
,
\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}
,
\lambda_{1}\lambda_{3}
,
\lambda_{2}\lambda_{3}
,
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3}
and any scalars on the left and right hand side to find

\gamma=\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{3}}\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\\
0\\
0\\
1\\
0\\
0\\
1\\
0\end{bmatrix}=\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{3}}\begin{bmatrix}e_{2}\\
e_{2}\\
e_{2}\end{bmatrix},

(11.13)

where
e_{2}=(0,1,0)\in\mathbb{C}^{3}
.

To find
D
, rearrange equation (
11.9
) in the following way.

\displaystyle v(\lambda)-\gamma

\displaystyle=D\lambda_{P}v(\lambda)~~\text{for}~\lambda\in\mathbb{D}^{3}

\displaystyle\iff\dfrac{1}{q(\lambda)}\begin{bmatrix}w(\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})\\
w(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{3})\\
w(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})\end{bmatrix}-\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{3}}\begin{bmatrix}e_{2}\\
e_{2}\\
e_{2}\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=D\lambda_{P}\bigg(\dfrac{1}{q(\lambda)}\bigg)\begin{bmatrix}w(\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})\\
w(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{3})\\
w(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})\end{bmatrix}~~\text{for}~\lambda\in\mathbb{D}^{3}

\displaystyle\iff~\sqrt{3}\begin{bmatrix}w(\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})\\
w(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{3})\\
w(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})\end{bmatrix}-q(\lambda)\begin{bmatrix}e_{2}\\
e_{2}\\
e_{2}\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{3}D\lambda_{P}\begin{bmatrix}w(\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})\\
w(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{3})\\
w(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})\end{bmatrix}~~\text{for}~\lambda\in\mathbb{D}^{3}.

Write
D
as a
9\times 9
matrix with entries
d_{ij}
for
i,j=1,\ldots,9
. Once more, expand out the equation above and compare each of the terms
\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}\lambda_{3}
,
\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}
,
\lambda_{1}\lambda_{3}
,
\lambda_{2}\lambda_{3}
,
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3}
and any scalars on the left and right hand side to find

D=\begin{bmatrix}\dfrac{1}{3}&0&0&-\dfrac{1}{6}&-\dfrac{1}{2}&-\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{6}}&-\dfrac{1}{6}&-\dfrac{1}{2}&-\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{6}}\\[14.22636pt]
0&\dfrac{1}{3}&\dfrac{1}{3\sqrt{6}}&\dfrac{1}{6}&-\dfrac{1}{6}&\dfrac{2}{3\sqrt{6}}&-\dfrac{1}{6}&-\dfrac{1}{6}&\dfrac{1}{3\sqrt{6}}\\[14.22636pt]
0&0&\dfrac{2}{9}&-\dfrac{1}{3\sqrt{6}}&\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{6}}&\dfrac{1}{9}&\dfrac{1}{3\sqrt{6}}&-\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{6}}&-\dfrac{1}{9}\\[14.22636pt]
-\dfrac{1}{6}&-\dfrac{1}{2}&-\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{6}}&\dfrac{1}{3}&0&0&-\dfrac{1}{6}&-\dfrac{1}{2}&\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{6}}\\[14.22636pt]
\dfrac{1}{18}&-\dfrac{1}{6}&\dfrac{1}{3\sqrt{6}}&-\dfrac{1}{9}&\dfrac{1}{3}&0&\dfrac{1}{18}&-\dfrac{1}{6}&-\dfrac{1}{3\sqrt{6}}\\[14.22636pt]
-\dfrac{3}{5\sqrt{6}}&\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{6}}&\dfrac{1}{5}&0&0&0&\dfrac{3}{5\sqrt{6}}&-\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{6}}&\dfrac{1}{5}\\[14.22636pt]
-\dfrac{1}{6}&-\dfrac{1}{2}&\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{6}}&-\dfrac{1}{6}&-\dfrac{1}{2}&\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{6}}&\dfrac{1}{3}&0&0\\[14.22636pt]
\dfrac{1}{18}&-\dfrac{1}{6}&-\dfrac{1}{3\sqrt{6}}&\dfrac{1}{18}&-\dfrac{1}{6}&-\dfrac{1}{3\sqrt{6}}&-\dfrac{1}{9}&\dfrac{1}{3}&0\\[14.22636pt]
-\dfrac{1}{3\sqrt{6}}&\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{6}}&-\dfrac{1}{9}&\dfrac{1}{3\sqrt{6}}&-\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{6}}&\dfrac{1}{9}&0&0&\dfrac{2}{9}\end{bmatrix}.

(11.14)

Hence
\varphi_{3}
, given by equation (
10.2
), can be written in the following form

\varphi_{3}(\lambda)=a+\langle\lambda_{P}(1_{\mathbb{C}^{9}}-D\lambda_{P})^{-1}\gamma,\beta\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{9}},~~\text{for all}~\lambda\in\mathbb{D}^{3},

where
a=0
and
\beta
,
\gamma
and
D
are defined by equations (
11.10
), (
11.13
) and (
11.14
) respectively and
\lambda_{P}
is defined by (
11.3
).
This can be verified by the computer program Maple.
